# OpalaTex & OpalaWebPage Project Design

This document describes the software architecture and project-level design decisions of **OpalaTex** (the MIT-licensed open-source client desktop application) and its connection to the optional **OpalaWebPage** static marketing site (hosted at `https://www.opalacoder.com`), which presents the project and links to a PayPal donation page. OpalaTex has no hosted Cloud AI service, no accounts, and no billing — the desktop app and the website are otherwise independent.

---

## 1. High-Level Architectural Diagram

The diagram below outlines the communication between the OpalaTex React/Vite front-end, its local Python backend, and the user's chosen AI provider (local Ollama or a third-party API).

```mermaid
graph TD
    subgraph Client [OpalaTex Client - Local Machine]
        FE[React/Vite Front-end] <-->|HTTP / WebSocket / streamed JSON events| BE[Python Local Server - ide_server.py]
        CLI[Terminal Front-end - cli.py / cli_render.py] <-->|in-process calls / same event stream| Bridge
        BE <-->|JSON IPC / task orchestration| Bridge[Agent Turn Engine - agent_stdin.py]
        Bridge <-->|AgenticBlocks Framework| Agent[Orchestrator Agent - memgpt_runtime.py]
    end

    BE <-->|LiteLLM| Provider[Local Ollama or user-configured third-party API]
    FE -->|Donation link only, no account or billing data| Web[OpalaWebPage - www.opalacoder.com]
    BE <-.->|opt-in project mirror: files, chats.json, device journals| Drive[(Google Drive - OpalaTex/project)]
    Mobile[OpalaTex Mobile - Android, mobile/] <-.->|drive.file: reads files and chats.json, writes its own journal| Drive
    Mobile -->|its own agent loop, user's API key| Provider
```

The Android app (§2.21) is not a third front-end of that engine: it has no connection to a desktop, and continues a project's conversations through the cloud mirror with an agent loop of its own.

There are two front-ends and **one** agent turn engine. The desktop window reaches
`agent_stdin.handle_run` over HTTP; the command-line interface calls it in process and
renders the same event stream to a terminal (§2.20).

---

## 2. OpalaTex Client Architecture (Desktop Application)

The client desktop application is a project-centric, AI-integrated LaTeX editor distributed under the MIT License. All local editor features remain available without registration, payment, or a Cloud account.

### 2.1 Backend / Core Components (`opalatex/` package)
- **Local HTTP/WS GUI Server (`opalatex/ide_server.py`)**: Runs an asynchronous HTTP server (default port `3000`). It serves the compiled React/Vite front-end and exposes local REST endpoints (`/api/*`) for workspace, IDE configuration, attachments, project chat storage, checkpoints, and agent execution.
- **Local session boundary** (`opalatex/local_auth.py`): the HTTP entry point validates Host, Origin and Fetch Metadata before routing requests. Every `/api/*` operation requires a random per-process session cookie, including native image/PDF/video requests and EventSource streams. In-process background-command tools receive the active server URL and cookie through `local_auth`, and perform loopback HTTP in a worker thread so they do not block the server's event loop. The cookie is HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict and scoped to `/api/`, with a port-specific name. `main.jsx` establishes and verifies it through a same-origin `POST /api/session` with a custom bootstrap header before mounting the app. There is no account, login or user prompt. Foreign origins receive neither CORS permission nor a bootstrap cookie; the application document also refuses foreign framing. Vite proxies the API and rewrites only its own same-origin Origin header to the backend origin; foreign origins remain intact and are rejected. `OPALATEX_BACKEND_URL` selects a different development backend when needed, defaulting to `http://localhost:3000`.
- **The port can change between launches, so nothing that must survive a restart lives in localStorage** (`find_available_port`, `ui_settings.json: last_active_project`, `/api/settings/last-project`): the server takes the first free port from 3000, and the page origin — the key localStorage is partitioned by — carries that port. A launch on 3001 (a second instance, or 3000 held by another program) therefore sees a different, older localStorage. That is how the window kept reopening an older project after a restart: `lastActiveProject` lived in localStorage, and the port probe bound without `SO_REUSEADDR`, so a port left with TIME_WAIT connections by the instance just closed looked taken and the reopened app moved to the next port, where an old value was waiting. The probe now sets `SO_REUSEADDR` on POSIX, matching asyncio's listener (not on Windows, where the flag would let it bind a port another process is listening on), and the last project is owned by the backend: `list-projects` returns `last_active_project`, and selecting a project posts it. The other interface preferences (theme, editor and terminal font, chat zoom and input history, floating-window geometry, ...) followed for the same reason: `gui_src/src/utils/preferences.js` keeps them in `ui_settings.json: client_prefs` (`/api/settings/client-prefs`) as string key/value pairs with localStorage's semantics. `main.jsx` awaits `hydratePreferences()` before mounting, so components still read synchronously from a cache; writes update the cache at once and are posted in ~200 ms batches, flushed with `keepalive` on `pagehide`. The first window to hydrate imports its old localStorage once (`client_prefs_imported`), never overwriting a key the backend already holds, so a later window on another port cannot bring back an older copy. New code must use `getPreference`/`setPreference`, not localStorage.
- **Slow request work runs off the event loop without reordering requests** (`AsyncHTTPServer.EXCLUSIVE_WORK_PATHS`, `_dispatch`): the server has one event loop, and a Tectonic compile (`/api/latex/compile`), `git status` (`/api/git/status`) and the project-tree walk (`/api/files`, polled every 10 s and on every window focus) used to run on it, freezing every streaming response — agent turns, terminal output — for their whole duration, seconds or minutes on Windows or on a first compile that fetches the TeX bundle. Their blocking part now runs in a worker thread while the request holds an exclusive-work `asyncio.Lock`, and **every other request waits on that lock before it is dispatched** (FIFO; an uncontended acquire does not yield). Requests are therefore still handled in arrival order and never interleave with that work, exactly as when it held the loop — a save that arrives during a compile still lands after it, which matters because `/api/file/write` is not atomic and Tectonic must not read a half-written file. Only work already in progress keeps flowing. Every other Git endpoint (`file-at-head`, `commit`, `diff`, `archive`, `restore`, `log`, `stage`, `discard`) is in the same set: its handler body runs in a worker thread through `_respond_from_thread`, which records the response the body decides and sends it from the loop, since the stream writer must not be touched from a thread. The front-end skips the periodic tree/status refresh while the window is hidden and coalesces the `focus` + `visibilitychange` pair that a return to the window fires. Agent tool calls were already running in threads (`FunctionBlock` → `asyncio.to_thread`) and are unaffected; events they publish (`latex_compiled`, `achievements_update`) reach the run streams through `AsyncHTTPServer.publish_event`, which records the event in a deque at once and has the loop deliver that deque in order (`call_soon_threadsafe` from a worker thread), because `asyncio.Queue` is not thread-safe and a scheduled put alone could be overtaken by the `tool_result` that follows. `get_file_tree` enumerates with `os.scandir`, which on Windows avoids the per-entry stat; its output is checked against the previous listdir/isdir walk, including symlinks, broken links, loops and unreadable directories (`tests/test_file_tree_scandir.py`). Saving the user's message and the final answer in `handle_run` (which also embeds them into archival memory, building the Chroma client and possibly downloading the embedding model on first use) goes through `agent_stdin._run_off_loop`, which keeps the inline call's cancellation timing: the call always completes and a cancel requested meanwhile lands at the turn's next await. Covered by `tests/test_exclusive_work_offload.py` and `tests/test_run_off_loop.py`.
- **Agent Orchestrator (`opalatex/memgpt_runtime.py`)**: Built on the vendored **AgenticBlocks** source package (`agenticblocks/`), which ships with OpalaTex rather than being installed as an external distribution. It implements a MemGPT-like memory architecture where the primary agent manages short-term and long-term memory, dispatches actions to modular "skills" (`opalatex/skills.py`), and exposes editor/file/document tools to the model.
- **JSON IPC Bridge (`opalatex/agent_stdin.py`)**: Owns the streamed agent run lifecycle for the IDE. It receives `/api/opalatex/run` payloads, persists user-visible chat history, normalizes attachments, coordinates pending GUI input requests, records agent activity for interruption/resume, and emits structured events back to the front-end.
- **LiteLLM / Tool-Call Compatibility Layer (`opalatex/litellm_compat.py`)**: Wraps AgenticBlocks LLM calls at the OpalaTex boundary. It sanitizes provider kwargs, repairs transport/history issues such as orphan tool messages and concatenated JSON tool calls, and adds bounded loop breakers for repeated schema validation failures. A repeated schema error disables only the offending tool for the rest of the current user turn; the other declared tools remain callable, so a bad `search_code` call cannot also suppress `read_file` or `create_plan`. The rejected call is never rewritten into a different action, and the breaker resets at the next user message. Function-backed tool schemas expose the callable's contract without appending the generic Python adapter implementation notes. It must not silently convert an invalid tool call into a different semantic action.
- **Project Store and Attachments**:
  - `opalatex/project.py`: Persists project metadata, chat history, chat branches, message attachments, core memory snapshots, and diagnostic agent activity.
  - **Non-negotiable invariant: thoughts persist in the chat interface.** Every persisted thought must remain accessible in its original chat and assistant turn after switching chats, reloading, or restarting, equally for every chat. Progress displays do not replace thoughts. Diagnostic activity (`thought`, `reflection`, and `stream_chunk`) is stored in `project_activity`, scoped by project and chat. `/api/chat/history` rehydrates the Thinking/Stream panel with every `thought`, `reflection` and `error` row, and applies its tail limit **only** to `stream_chunk` (`ProjectStore.list_activity(truncate_events=...)`). A single flat limit over all events is what let a long streamed answer displace the entire thinking phase; dropping the limit outright instead loads every per-token row the chat ever persisted on each open, so the cap stays but can no longer reach a thought. `thought` activity is also associated back to the matching assistant turn in the chat UI as a collapsed "AI Thoughts" details block, while assistant chat message content remains limited to the visible final response. Only explicit history removal or superseding an edited turn may remove its thoughts from that view.
  - `opalatex/archival.py`: Archival memory over ChromaDB (`<opalatex home>/chroma`), one collection per project. It supplies its own embedding function instead of taking Chroma's default, for two reasons: Chroma's `DefaultEmbeddingFunction` constructs a fresh `ONNXMiniLM_L6_V2` — and therefore a fresh `InferenceSession` — on **every** call, and ONNX Runtime pins each intra-op thread with `pthread_setaffinity_np` unless the thread count is set explicitly, which strict Snap confinement denies with one error line per thread per session. The replacement caches one session for the process and requests an explicit `intra_op_num_threads` (`OPALATEX_EMBEDDING_THREADS`; `0` restores Chroma's untuned default). **It must subclass `DefaultEmbeddingFunction`, not `ONNXMiniLM_L6_V2`**, so `name()` stays `"default"`: Chroma persists that name in the collection configuration and refuses to open a collection with a different one, so subclassing the ONNX class directly breaks every project whose collection already exists. Any failure to build it falls back to Chroma's default rather than leaving the project unable to embed.
  - `opalatex/attachments.py`: Converts uploaded images and supported documents into normalized descriptors, extracts document text when possible, caches the original of a supported document on disk (`<opalatex home>/attachment_cache`, addressed by content hash) so the descriptor carries a `raw_path` instead of the base64 original, and avoids forwarding unsupported binary payloads to text-only models.
- **VCS & Compilation Managers**:
  - `opalatex/vcs.py`: Implements user-facing Git features and internal shadow checkpoints around agent turns. Mutating file tools do not create their own checkpoints; each participating agent run, including ephemeral `run_skill` workers, creates labeled start/end checkpoints only when needed.
  - `opalatex/latex_compiler.py`: Handles compiling LaTeX using Tectonic (`tectonic` CLI), supporting full, partial (chapter/file), and fast single-pass draft compilation (`tectonic -X compile` with `-r 0`).
  - `synctex_parser.py`: Maps PDF rendering view back to the corresponding LaTeX lines.
  - `opalatex/external_tools.py`: Locates and installs the external tools (Tectonic, pandoc). Lookup order is `<opalatex home>/bin` (in-app installs, first so a reinstall replaces the tool in use), then the bundled `bin/` (source checkout, PyInstaller, `$SNAP/bin`), then `PATH`. **Downloads go to `<opalatex home>/bin`, never next to the package**: `site-packages` is a read-only squashfs in the snap, which is why *Install Pandoc* and reinstalling Tectonic failed there with `[Errno 30] Read-only file system`. Only the named executable is extracted from an archive, through a temporary file and `os.replace`, so a running binary can be replaced. The snap stages Debian's `pandoc`, which reads its data files from a compiled-in `/usr/share/pandoc` that is empty inside confinement; the launcher exports `pandoc_datadir=$SNAP/usr/share/pandoc` (the Cabal override for that path) and the build refuses to prime without the data. DOCX export failures are reported in a dialog, not in the PDF preview's compile-error panel, which is collapsed until the first compile and would hide them. **The shells the app starts reach the same tools** (`environment_with_managed_tools`): `run_command`, `run_python_script` and the integrated terminal (`terminal_manager.py`, both platforms) get a `PATH` with the directories of the tectonic and pandoc that `find_tool` resolved prepended, in `find_tool`'s order. Without it a shell could not run the Tectonic the IDE compiles with, since neither `<opalatex home>/bin` nor a source checkout's `bin/` is normally on `PATH`; skill scripts that look the engine up by `PATH` (`tex-to-jpt/scripts/tikz_to_image.py`) find an in-app install the same way.
- **Snippet Translation (`opalatex/translation.py`)**: A provider-neutral translation of a text excerpt, used by the *Translate selection* action in the PDF viewer and the Markdown preview. See §2.13.
- **Snippet Look-up (`opalatex/snippet_query.py`)**: A one-off question about a selected excerpt, answered in a card of its own by an ephemeral agent with read-only access to the project; never a chat turn. Used by *Look up the selection…* in the PDF viewer, the Markdown preview and the chat. See §2.13.4.
- **Dictation (`opalatex/transcription.py`, `opalatex/transcription_config.py`)**: Transcribes a recording from the chat composer with a Whisper model running on this machine. See §2.13.3.
- **Snippet Pronunciation (`opalatex/speech.py`, `opalatex/speech_config.py`, `opalatex/voice_store.py`)**: Reads a text excerpt aloud, either from a Piper voice downloaded into `<opalatex home>/voices/` and run in-process, or through the OpenAI speech contract against a hosted provider or a local server. Used by the *Pronounce selection* action in the PDF viewer, the Markdown preview and the chat. See §2.13.1.
- **Image Generation (`opalatex/image_gen_config.py`)**: Global settings (enabled, model, size, output folder) for the `generate_image` tool, which produces project files through the provider-neutral `ImageGenerationBlock` of the vendored AgenticBlocks. See §2.12.
- **Document Export Tools**:
  - `create_docx_file` and `create_pptx_file` are exposed through the agent tool registry for generated Word and PowerPoint artifacts. They should be used instead of asking an agent to write raw binary office files.
  - `create_presentation`, `edit_presentation` and `check_presentation` (backed by `opalatex/jpt/`) are the same idea for the IDE's own presentation format — see §2.19.
- **Monitor Agents (`opalatex/monitors/`)**: Background watchers that notify the user or wake a chat, run by a per-user daemon that outlives the window and starts at login. See §2.24.
- **Cloud Project Mirroring (`opalatex/cloud/`)**: An opt-in, per-project mirror of a project to cloud storage, behind a provider-neutral facade (`CloudStorageProvider`). Disabled by default; nothing leaves the machine until a user turns it on for a specific project. See §2.15.

### Project user prompt prefix

Project settings include a separate **Prompt prefix** tab (English and Brazilian Portuguese). `ProjectData.user_prompt_prefix` is persisted in the projects table with an empty default and migrated for existing databases. The update endpoint validates it as text; listing and saving settings preserve it verbatim, including whitespace. Clearing the field disables it.

Both AgenticBlocks agent types expose the general-purpose `user_prompt_prefix` field. At each completion boundary, shared `prepend_user_prompt` adds it to the first user message in a request copy, preserving multimodal parts and tool-call ordering; requests without a user message receive one after leading system/developer messages. The prefix never enters persisted conversation or editable core memory and is reapplied after eviction, on heartbeats, summarization and final synthesis. OpalaTex snapshots it into orchestrators and workers when constructed. Saved changes apply to newly constructed runs; an already running agent retains its snapshot. These are user-role instructions, not a guarantee of model compliance or an override of system instructions.

The prefix reaches the **chat agents only**. Snippet translation (§`opalatex/translation.py`) and prompt evolution (`_execute_prompt_evolution`) are single-shot transforms whose entire input is the user's payload, and `prepend_user_prompt` fuses the prefix into that same user message with no boundary between instruction and payload: the translator translated the prefix and the popup displayed it as the translation, and prompt evolution rewrote it into the refined prompt. A prefix such as "always answer in Portuguese" would also contradict the requested target language. Neither call site takes a prefix, and neither consults the project.

### 2.2 Offline by Default & Open-Source (MIT)
The OpalaTex repository contains no cloud proxy calls, no account tracking, and no credit-meter logic. All editing and AI features function via Ollama and user-configured third-party providers (API key + optional base URL). The previous private `OpalaTexCloud` overlay project and its `opalatex_cloud` extension package have been discontinued and are no longer built or referenced from this repository.

Project mirroring to cloud storage (§2.15) is the one network path the application itself can open, and it is **opt-in per project**: it transfers nothing until a user selects a provider, authorizes it, and enables it for that project. There is still no OpalaTex account, no hosted service, and no telemetry — the credentials are the user's own and the files go to the user's own storage.

### 2.3 Open-Source Support and Donations
- **License**: The repository includes the standard MIT License in `LICENSE`, matching `pyproject.toml` metadata.
- **Desktop donation UI**: Settings > About displays a PayPal donation button and the QR Code from `gui_src/public/qr-code.png`.
- **Compiled asset**: Vite copies the QR Code to `opalatex/gui/qr-code.png` for packaged desktop builds.
- **Separation from features**: Donations support project maintenance and do not unlock any additional application features.

### 2.3.1 Installation, Uninstallation, and Feedback

- **Direct-install uninstallers**: Packaged releases include `uninstall.sh` or `uninstall.ps1`. The Unix installer exposes `opalatex-uninstall`; the Windows installer registers a per-user entry in Installed Apps and creates a Start-menu shortcut. A release predating the bundled uninstaller triggers a fallback fetch, which addresses the repository at `HEAD` so it resolves the default branch rather than a hardcoded branch name that 404s whenever the release branch moves. **That fetch is never fatal**: the application payload is already unpacked by then, so a failure warns and installation completes, and the uninstall entry points are published only when the script is actually present — Windows never offers an uninstall command that cannot run. Default removal deletes only application-owned binaries, links, shortcuts, and exact PATH entries. Global data and every project directory are preserved. Purging global data is a separate explicit, confirmed option, and project directories are never purge targets.
- **The Windows uninstaller removes the application files first and never fails silently** (`uninstall.ps1`): launched from Installed apps or the Start menu it owns a console window that closes when the script ends, so a bare `throw` vanished unread and users reported an app that "will not uninstall". Every refusal or failure goes through `Stop-Uninstall` (a script-level `trap` catches the unexpected ones): it is written to `%TEMP%\OpalaTex-uninstall.log` and kept on screen until dismissed, unless `-Yes` asked for an unattended run. Before anything is removed it refuses while any process runs from the installation directory — not only `OpalaTex.exe` but the `QtWebEngineProcess`, `tectonic`, `pandoc` and `winpty-agent` it starts, which lock their files. It moves the *process* working directory out (`[Environment]::CurrentDirectory`; `Set-Location` alone moves only PowerShell's location, and the Start-menu shortcut starts in the application directory, which Windows then refuses to delete). The application directory is removed **before** PATH, shortcuts, the Installed-apps entry and any purge, retried for transient antivirus locks; if it cannot be removed the script stops with all of those intact, so the installation stays visible and can be uninstalled again, and no data is purged for an uninstall that did not happen. It ends by naming the folder that holds the preserved global data.
- **The Windows installer** (`install.ps1`) silences PowerShell progress only inside the download function and the extraction block — Windows PowerShell 5.1 redraws the bar per chunk and per file, which makes `Invoke-WebRequest` and `Expand-Archive` many times slower on a release this size, and a script-level assignment would leak into the user's session, since the script runs through `irm | iex`. It prints the download size instead, and refuses to extract over a copy that is running from the installation directory, which would otherwise fail on locked executables partway through and leave mixed versions behind.
- **The Linux release must start on a stock desktop, and the build proves it** (`scripts/check_bundle_libraries.py`, called by `build_exe.sh` after PyInstaller; `build.yml` installs the libraries on the runner): PyInstaller copies a dependency from the build machine and only warns (`Library not found`) when that machine lacks it. Qt's xcb platform plugin (`libqxcb.so` via `libQt6XcbQpa`) links nine X11 client libraries — `libxcb-cursor`, `-icccm`, `-image`, `-keysyms`, `-render-util`, `-shape`, `-util`, `-xkb` and `libxkbcommon-x11` — that the `ubuntu-latest` runner image does not carry, so releases shipped without them and aborted on Linux Mint 22 / Ubuntu 24.04 with "no Qt platform plugin could be initialized" until the user apt-installed them; reproduced in a clean `ubuntu:24.04` container under Xvfb, where the same bundle starts once those nine libraries are in `_internal/`. `install.sh` deliberately does not install system packages (it needs no administrator rights), so the fix is to bundle them: the release job apt-installs the nine packages before building, and the gate runs `ldd` with the bootloader's `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` on what opening the window loads (the xcb and Wayland platform plugins, the xcb GL integrations, `QtWebEngineProcess`, `QtWebEngineWidgets`, `libpython`) and fails on any dependency that resolves outside the bundle or not at all. Libraries PyInstaller excludes on purpose — glibc, the GL/EGL/DRM driver stack, `libxcb.so.1`, `libxcb-dri*`, `libwayland-*` — belong to the user's graphics stack; the gate asks `PyInstaller.depend.dylib` which those are instead of copying its list. A startup binary missing from the bundle is itself a failure, so a Qt layout change cannot make the gate pass by checking nothing.
- **Under snap, user-facing default locations are the real home** (`config.user_home_dir`, `config.expand_user_path`): inside a snap `~` is `$SNAP_USER_DATA` = `~/snap/opalatex/<revision>`, private to one revision. Projects are stored by absolute path, so a project created there (the folder picker's default was the data directory's parent, and the onboarding pilot is `~/OpalaTexPilot`) pointed after the next refresh at the previous revision's directory, which AppArmor makes read-only, and creating a file in it failed. The folder picker's default and `~` in `create-project` resolve to `SNAP_REAL_HOME` under snap (unchanged elsewhere). `create-project` validates the folder by creating a probe file (`config.check_data_dir`), not with `os.access`, which AppArmor denials do not affect.
- **The snap's Python can create virtual environments** (`snapcraft.yaml`): `$SNAP/bin` leads PATH, so `python` in the integrated terminal is the app's own interpreter, a `venv --copies` made at build time by the gnome SDK's `python3`. venv records that interpreter as `home` in `pyvenv.cfg`, but the SDK is a build-only snap, so `python -m venv .venv` linked `.venv/bin/python` to a missing file and failed with `[Errno 2] No such file or directory`. The copied binary already loads its standard library from the base snap's `/usr`, so `override-build` rewrites `home` to `/usr/bin` after the pip installs. That standard library has no `ensurepip`, and Debian's `ensurepip` reads pip's wheel from the absolute `/usr/share/python-wheels`, so two layouts bind the staged `python3.12-venv` copies at those paths, and `override-prime` refuses to build a snap that lacks either. A venv made this way links to `/usr/bin/python3` inside confinement and is meant to be used from OpalaTex's terminal; the host's `/usr/bin/python3` may be a different version.
- **Snap escape guidance**: `GET /api/app/environment` reports the runtime platform and whether the backend is inside Snap. Settings > About shows direct-install instructions only for Snap, with a copyable command that must be run from a system terminal. The application never attempts to escape confinement or run the host installation itself. Direct installation grants the normal user's permissions, not unrestricted root access, and does not migrate or delete Snap data.
- **User-controlled feedback**: Settings > About builds a pre-filled URL for the public GitHub issue form containing only the app version, platform, distribution type, and placeholder prose. The existing external-URL endpoint opens it in the system browser. No issue is created and no diagnostic or user content is transmitted until the user reviews and submits the GitHub form.

### 2.4 Rich Text Editor & Math Rendering (`gui_src/src/components/RichTextEditor.jsx`)

- **Saving acknowledges a document snapshot** (`App.jsx`, `utils/fileSave.js`): writes are serialized per project and file. A response updates the disk baseline, but replaces the live buffer only if it still equals the submitted text and belongs to the same project/document. Later typing and other tabs survive delayed responses, including JPT canonicalization. Switching projects invalidates old UI receipts; overlapping saves keep the saving indicator active until all writes finish. Regression tests drive the actual save handler with deferred HTTP responses.

- **Overleaf-style Rich Text mode**: Parses LaTeX source into structured blocks; editable prose blocks (headings, paragraphs, lists, quotes) are rendered as `contentEditable` elements. Non-editable blocks (math, figures, tables, code) are rendered as read-only previews with "jump to source" on click.
- **Environment recursion is the default, with a named blacklist** (`latexBlockParser.js: OPAQUE_ENV_NAMES`): an environment's body is parsed as a sub-document unless it is listed as one that is not — the verbatim family, cell/plot grammars (`array`, `tabbing`, matrices, pgfplots `axis`), or content for another consumer (`filecontents`). It used to be the other way round: only `frame` and the beamer titled boxes recursed, and every other wrapper became one opaque read-only blob. That made the loss **compound** — a `columns`, `minipage` or `theorem` swallowed the `itemize`s, figures and TikZ pictures inside it, all of which the parser handles perfectly well on their own, so one unrecognized wrapper cost every supported construct nested within it. Unrecognized environments now become an `envblock` container whose arguments are consumed into its header (`\begin{minipage}[t]{0.5\textwidth}`) so they never leak into the body as stray text. Argument scanning deliberately does not skip whitespace, so a brace group on the next line stays body content; when the guess is wrong the source is still safe, because both editors preserve the header verbatim — only the header/body split shifts.
- **Font declarations render their effect, not their markup** (`gui_src/src/utils/latexFontDeclarations.js`, shared by both editors so a declaration never renders one way in one mode and another in the other): `\Huge`, `\small`, `\bfseries`, `\itshape` and the rest of the standard set take no argument and apply to the end of their enclosing scope, so they are not found by the argument-taking `\textbf{x}` path and used to leak into the preview as literal text — a slide title read `{\Huge\bfseries Bem-vindos}` instead of large bold text. Both spellings are handled: a scoping group (`{\Huge\bfseries ...}`) and a bare declaration running to the end of its scope (`\footnotesize Referência: ...`). The whitespace terminating a declaration belongs to it, since TeX consumes it as the control word's terminator. Sizes are emitted as `em`, which compounds when nested where LaTeX's are absolute relative to `\normalsize` — accepted, as nesting two sizes is rare. A brace group that does *not* open with a declaration is handled by the rule below.
- **Command arguments are matched by counting braces** (`gui_src/src/utils/latexInlineCommands.js`, shared): the `\cmd{arg}` → `arg` reduction used a `\{([^}]*)\}` regex, which ends the argument at the first `}` and therefore cannot handle an argument containing markup of its own. On `\footnote{o termo \textit{modelo} ...}` that left a stray `\textit{` mid-sentence and an orphaned `}` at the end — corrupted output rather than merely unrendered markup, and it affected every command with nested braces (`\caption`, `\alert`, ...). All scanning now counts braces.
- **Notes render as a marker, not as body text**: `\footnote` (with `\footnotetext`, `\thanks`, `\marginpar`) compiles to a marker in the text and a note at the foot of the page, so reducing it to its argument the way `\alert{x}` reduces to `x` would splice the note into the middle of the sentence. Both editors show a superscript `†` carrying the note's text on hover. The marker is deliberately not a number: LaTeX assigns those at compile time from a document-wide counter, and inventing one could be mistaken for the real numbering. The whole command is kept verbatim, so an optional argument (`\footnote[3]{...}`) survives; editing the note text means going to the source.
- **Braces that only group render away but are never written away** (`gui_src/src/utils/latexBraceGroups.js`, also shared): a brace group whose content holds no control sequence and no comment does nothing but group, so `1{,}5` — the standard decimal comma, braced so it is an ordinary symbol rather than punctuation — reads as `1,5`. The braces are kept in the source, because they can carry meaning the rendering does not show: `sha{f}{f}le` is how an "ff" ligature is broken. A group containing a command is preserved whole, rendering included, since what it means depends on the command. The unwrap uses a lookbehind to skip command arguments, so `\ref{x}` is safe regardless of where the rule sits among the other substitutions, and it must run *before* `\{`/`\}` are unescaped or a literal brace pair would be stripped as if it were a group.
- **Beamer `columns` are laid out as columns**: `columns` splits on `\column{width}` markers and on nested `column` environments (both spellings occur in the wild and are recognized in the same pass), producing `columns`/`column` blocks that render side by side with the declared width fractions. A width in absolute units has no fraction to honour and shares the remaining space evenly; the row wraps to a stack in a narrow pane. `latexBlockSerializer.js: RESPLICED_BLOCK_TYPES` lists every container type that must be re-spliced rather than emitted from its captured `source` — omitting one silently drops edits made inside it.
- **KaTeX rendering**: Inline and display math use KaTeX's MathML output consistently across Markdown chat content, confirmation dialogs, the rich-text editor, and the presentation editor's equation elements (§2.18). The rich-text editor renders through a **persistent Worker pool** (`katexRenderWorker.js`), whose workers are reused across equations to avoid per-equation module re-initialization cost.
- **Uniform math output**: Every mathematical surface uses the same MathML representation. No expression-specific HTML/MathML routing is performed, and fenced `latex` or `tex` blocks are promoted to display math only after KaTeX validates them.
- **Lazy block rendering**: An `IntersectionObserver`-based `LazyBlock` wrapper mounts block content only when near the viewport (`rootMargin: 800px`), preventing all equations from rendering simultaneously on mount.
- **Throttled scroll**: `handleScroll` uses `requestAnimationFrame` to batch scroll-driven layout reads.
- **Precomputed line offsets**: Line-start offsets are cached via `useMemo` and `sourceLineFromOffset` uses binary search (O(log n)) instead of string slicing (O(n)) per block.
- **KB article**: See `docs/kb/katex_equations_longtime.md` for the full diagnosis and resolution of the equation rendering freeze issue.

### 2.5 LaTeX WYSIWYG Mode (`gui_src/src/wysiwyg/`)
A second, model-based document editor for `.tex` files, offered alongside Rich Text mode as a per-file toggle in the editor toolbar (the two are mutually exclusive — they are different editors over the same file). It is lazy-loaded, since it pulls in the ProseMirror stack.

Where Rich Text mode renders blocks and lets the user edit the LaTeX text inside prose blocks, this mode maintains a **real document model** (a ProseMirror schema for a bounded LaTeX subset) and edits *that*: bold is a mark rather than a typed `\textbf{}`, Enter creates an `\item`, Tab nests a sublist, and undo/redo belong to the editor.

- **The `.tex` file remains the single source of truth.** The model is derived from it and serialized back on every change; nothing else persists.
- **Untouched blocks are written back byte-for-byte.** Every node records the source slice it came from (`raw`), the text separating it from its next sibling (`tail`), and the whitespace surrounding its inline content (`head`/`foot`). Together these cover every byte of the file. Before rebuilding a node from the model, `toLatex.js` compares it against a *pristine* snapshot taken at parse time; identical nodes emit their original bytes and the model is never consulted. This is the invariant that makes the mode safe on version-controlled LaTeX — editing one word touches one paragraph, instead of reformatting the whole file on first save.
- **Nothing is dropped.** Any construct outside the modelled subset — unknown environments, user macros, `\cite`/`\ref`, figures, tables, TikZ, the preamble — becomes a `latex_raw` block atom or an `inline_raw` inline atom carrying its source, preserved verbatim and rendered read-only. Verbatim blocks reuse the preview components exported from `RichTextEditor.jsx`, so a figure looks the same in both modes.
- **Whitespace is the one deliberate normalization.** The model has no concept of source line wrapping, so runs of whitespace inside a paragraph collapse to a single space. Hard wrapping survives until the user edits a given paragraph, and is lost on that paragraph when they do.
- **Module layout**: `schema.js` (the node/mark schema and the command↔mark tables), `inline.js` (inline LaTeX ↔ marks/atoms, with a bijective escape and ligature table), `fromLatex.js` (blocks from `latexBlockParser.js` → model), `toLatex.js` (model → source, verbatim-preserving), `commands.js` (editing commands; the list algorithms are reproduced from `prosemirror-schema-list`, which is not a project dependency), `nodeViews.jsx` (verbatim previews and editable math), `LatexWysiwygEditor.jsx` (the surface).
- **Conformance suite** (`gui_src/src/wysiwyg/__tests__`, run with `npm run test:wysiwyg` — `node --test`, no added dependencies): byte-exact round-trips over a corpus including the real SIBGRAPI paper template read straight from `templates/`, edit-locality properties (typing in a paragraph, editing a frame title, deleting a block, splitting a paragraph), and the LaTeX produced by each editing command. Round-trip exactness is tested before anything else: an editor that normalizes the file on open is unusable for LaTeX regardless of what else it does.
- **Inline scopes are marks** (`scope`), covering the three shapes that occur: braces carrying font declarations (`{\Huge\bfseries x}`), a bare declaration running to the end of its scope (`\small x`), and braces that only group (`1{,}5`). The mark carries the declaration run verbatim in `prefix` (empty for a grouping-only pair) plus whether the source braced it and how deeply it was nested. A `key` attribute distinguishes one braced group from the next, so adjacent groups are never merged — `sha{f}{f}le` must not become `sha{ff}le`. The mark sets `excludes: ''`, since a mark type excludes itself by default and a nested declaration would otherwise replace its enclosing one. Two constraints shape how it is written back: ProseMirror keeps same-type marks in insertion order rather than source order, so the serializer restores nesting by comparing `depth`; and a declaration that came from inside another mark's scope is always braced, because ProseMirror's rank ordering brings it back out ahead of that mark, where writing it bare would extend it over the rest of the paragraph. `\textbf{\small x}` therefore returns as `{\small \textbf{x}}` — the same scope, not the same bytes. Like every other re-serialization this reaches only a paragraph the user edited.
- **Wrappers**: the same recursion policy as §2.4 applies, since both modes share `latexBlockParser.js`. Unrecognized environments become `env_block` nodes and beamer columns become `columns_block`/`column_block`, all three keeping their opening and closing text verbatim in `headerRaw`/`footerRaw` — the uniform way to preserve `\begin{minipage}[t]{0.5\textwidth}` and a marker-style `\column{0.55\textwidth}`, which has no closing text at all.
- **Known gaps**: tables and figures are preserved but not yet editable in the model (`prosemirror-tables` is available for a follow-up); `\newcommand` is not expanded for display; cross-references and citations are not resolved against the `.aux` file, so they render as source chips rather than numbers.

### 2.6 Agent Run, Streaming, and Human Approval Flow
- **Run entrypoint**: The React front-end posts to `/api/opalatex/run`. The local server streams structured JSON events back to the UI so chat messages, thoughts, tool calls, problems, cancellation, and final responses can update incrementally.
- **Live-output rendering contract**: While an agent is running, visible response chunks are appended immediately as raw, pre-wrapped text in the chat and Output panels. Markdown and KaTeX are never parsed on partial output. When the final `agent_response` arrives, the temporary raw stream is replaced by the completed assistant message and only then rendered as Markdown/KaTeX, including bounded LaTeX-delimiter normalization for provider-specific final output. Model streaming defaults to enabled unless a project explicitly opts out. The run stream is read by `utils/frameBatchedLines.js`: the backend writes one event per token, and the lines read before a frame are handled together, in order, so a frame's state updates land in one render instead of one render of the whole application per token (a timeout stands in for `requestAnimationFrame` in a hidden window, and lines still waiting when the stream ends or fails are handled before the read loop returns). The live reasoning tail (`utils/thoughtTail.js`) appends to an array its successive states share instead of copying its window per chunk, and its text is joined once per render.
- **A live turn belongs to the chat it started in** (`App.jsx: runChatIdRef`/`isLiveTurnOnScreen`): the user may switch chats while a turn runs. Its running bubble, streamed reasoning and answer, final `agent_response`/`cancelled`/`error` bubbles, `token_usage` measurement and Agent Thinking panel entries are shown only while that chat is on screen; the backend persists all of them in the turn's own chat, so returning to it reloads them. Every change of active chat empties the Agent Thinking panel (one effect on `activeChatId`, since new chats, task chats, edited-message branches and the tutorial all set the id without reloading a history), and a history response for a chat the user already left is discarded.
- **Final-response and tool-call contract**: Only provider-native `tool_calls` execute actions, and a final response is preserved verbatim, including JSON and Markdown examples; plain-text JSON is never recovered or executed as a tool call. **There is exactly one user-facing text channel**, assistant `content` (`MemGPTAgentBlock`): every non-empty text a run produces is delivered, in order, whether or not tool calls travelled in the same message. The turn continues while a tool call is pending — the result has to be delivered — or while the model calls `new_heartbeat`; a reply that does neither ends the turn and is the answer. See the entry below.
- **An attached image must not cost the agent its ability to act** (`agenticblocks/blocks/llm/memgpt_agent.py: _acompletion`): a multimodal request used to be rewritten from `ollama_chat/<model>` to `ollama/<model>`, because Ollama's native `/api/chat` did not accept OpenAI-style `image_url` parts while the OpenAI-compatible route did. **That reroute became the more expensive bug.** On the `ollama/` route a streamed tool call never arrives as `tool_calls` at all: the provider emits it as plain text, one token at a time (`{"`, `name`, `":"`, …), so `stream_chunk_builder` has nothing to assemble and the model's action reaches the caller as prose. Measured against `ollama_chat/kimi-k3:cloud`: 93 chunks, **zero** carrying `delta.tool_calls`, the whole call rebuilt as the string `{"name":"get_editor_state","arguments":{}}`. So an agent that was sent a screenshot could not act for the entire turn — it could only announce what it would have done, which is exactly what the user saw and reported. **The condition was one variable, and it took a controlled pair to find it**: the same model, prompt and toolset issued five native tool calls with streaming and no image, and none with an image; the earlier suspects (the model's tool-calling ability, thinking, the size of the prompt, the number of tools) each survived a test and were wrong. LiteLLM converts image parts for the native route now — verified against 1.97, where the image is described correctly *and* the tool call arrives native while streaming — so the reroute buys nothing and costs tool calling, and requests go to the model's own route. `tests/test_multimodal_route.py` is the guard against reintroducing it; `requirements.txt` was carrying a stale `litellm>=1.40.0` against `pyproject.toml`'s `>=1.95.0`, and this behaviour is the reason the floor matters.
- **One text channel, because two of them silently ate the deliverable** (`agenticblocks/blocks/llm/memgpt_agent.py`, `HEARTBEAT_TOOL_NAME` / `max_idle_heartbeats`): the runtime used to have two places to put user-facing text — assistant `content` and a `send_message` tool — and preferred the wrong one without saying so. **Two measured traces, same request, same project, different models.** `ollama_chat/kimi-k3:cloud` wrote the whole 6 600-character answer into the `content` of a message that also carried a tool call and put a 415-character pointer ("roteiro entregue acima") into `send_message`; the loop only inspected `content` when there were *no* tool calls, so the answer was dropped and the pointer delivered — the user replied "Não vi nenhum roteiro", and was right. `ollama_chat/glm-5.3-flash:cloud` put the full 8 440-character answer into one `send_message`, then, obeying a prompt rule to close the turn with text, added a second one summarising it; `response_mode="last"` kept the summary and discarded the answer. **Neither model misbehaved**: each followed the contract as written, and the runtime lost the deliverable while reporting success. `openai/gpt-5.6-luna` and `gemini/gemini-3.7-flash` were untouched by both defects for one reason — they emit empty `content` alongside tool calls and one message per turn — so the bug read as model-specific while being structural. The two defects were independent (the kimi turn issued a single `send_message`, so no `response_mode` could have saved it), which is why neither knob alone was the fix. **`send_message` is gone**, from the framework as well as from OpalaTex. The delegation path needed the same repair and did not get it for free: `run_skill` spawns an `LLMAgentBlock`, which returned only the content of the final tool-free response, so a worker that wrote its report beside a tool call and then said "report delivered above" handed the orchestrator the pointer and dropped the report. An early reading of this — that the worker "already had" the single channel because it has no `send_message` — was wrong: having no delivery tool is not the same as not discarding co-emitted text. Both blocks now compose their output through one shared rule, `compose_visible_response`, so they cannot drift. `content` is delivered always; `new_heartbeat` is the one way to hold a turn open without acting, needed only to speak now and act next, since an ordinary tool call already continues the run. **The failure mode inverts, and that is the point**: a model that does not follow the rite now ends its turn early, visibly, and the user says "continue" — instead of losing the answer in silence. It also stops fighting the convention every provider already implements, which is why the two models that worked, worked. `max_heartbeats` remains a runaway guardrail (default 50, `_CORE_AGENT_DEFAULTS`), and `max_idle_heartbeats` (default 2) bounds the only thing that can still spin: an unbroken run of `new_heartbeat` with no work in between. Spending that allowance must not deliver the failure it exists to stop, so it ends in an explicit request for the answer (`force_final_answer`: `tool_choice="none"` plus an instruction to state what was actually established and, failing that, to say plainly that it could not act) rather than promoting the last announcement. The count resets on every real tool call, since speak-act-speak-act is ordinary work. A response that is all reasoning and no action spends the same allowance: it is the same model-did-not-act step, and the branch handling it drops the message so the model never reads its own attempt back and writes it again — one observed trace (`ollama_chat/kimi-k3:cloud`, issuing no native tool call at all) burned 27 calls that way; it now costs three and ends with no answer rather than a false one, which is what tells the user the model is the problem. **Two dead ends are worth recording.** A plan-mode lock that detected the missing `create_plan` and re-ran the agent could not fix anything: every retry hit the same premature termination, fighting the symptom with the weapon that caused it. And `model_controlled_turn_end`, the previous fix, was correct about the disease — the runtime could not tell an answer from an announcement, so "I will now read the file and draft the plan" ended the turn — but its cure created the second channel that lost the answers above; `new_heartbeat` expresses the same intention without one. `AgentOutput.termination_reason` reports why a run stopped, which is the only thing that distinguishes a turn the model ended from one the runtime cut short.
- **Progress is folded, never filtered** (`agent_stdin.py: _fold_turn_progress`, `memgpt_runtime.py: _strip_progress_fence`, `formatMessage.jsx` ```` ```progress ```` fence): with one text channel the model speaks while it works *and* when it is done, and both reach the chat — measured on a real turn, nine progress lines sat above the 6 812-character deliverable in one bubble. `MemGPTAgentBlock` therefore reports the same text three ways: `AgentOutput.response` is the whole thing joined (the lossless default, so a caller that ignores the split keeps every word), plus `narration` and `final_text` as a view of it. The orchestrator's chat message wraps the progress in a ```` ```progress ```` fence the GUI renders as a collapsed block, so the answer is what the reader meets first. **Two guards keep the layout from becoming a filter.** The fold is computed on the *sanitized* string and only where the boundary is still verifiable in it (`endswith(final_text)`, no fence inside the progress); if a correction rewrote the tail the response is returned whole, because showing progress unfolded is a cosmetic loss while dropping it is the defect the protocol was rewritten to remove. And `seed_chat_orchestrator_history` strips the fence before replaying an assistant turn to the model: the markup is the host's, not the model's, and a model reads its own last turn as an example — the empty-response marker taught that lesson the expensive way. Only the progress chatter is stripped, never the answer, and a restored `agent_state` keeps the chatter verbatim anyway.
- **Verbatim means verbatim, and two places were reshaping it** (`agent.py: compose_visible_response`, `memgpt_agent.py` reasoning split): the join used to `strip()` each part, and the MemGPT loop reassigned `content` from `split_inline_reasoning` even when no `<think>` tag was found — and that splitter strips what it returns. Either one turned a Markdown indented code block into ordinary prose, contradicting the contract this protocol is built on. Parts are now kept exactly as written, separated by a blank line, and only a wholly blank part is dropped; the reasoning split runs only when reasoning was actually found, which is how `LLMAgentBlock` had always guarded it. The fold boundary is likewise compared raw rather than stripped, so an answer that opens with indentation keeps it instead of donating it to the progress block.
- **Spending the budget ends in a request for the answer, not a break** (`memgpt_agent.py: _EXHAUSTED_BUDGET_ALERT`, `AgentOutput.budget_exhausted`): the loop used to `break` in the same iteration whose tool batch spent the last heartbeat, so the exhausted-budget branch — tool calls off, "provide the final user-facing response" — was unreachable in the ordinary flow, and the model never read the results of its last step. Traced in real chats: the slide compiled, the rendered page was checked, the model spent its last heartbeat recording progress, and the user got "Vou registrar o progresso:" with a cut-short notice for finished work. The run now makes that one request: tool calls off, and an alert asking for an honest status (what was done and verified, and what is still unfinished) rather than a success report, because the budget can run out mid-work. Whatever returns ends the run; tool calls returned anyway (a provider may ignore `tool_choice="none"`) are refused with a `Not executed` result each — a call left without one breaks the next request built from the history — and never run. A correction for an empty response that spends the last heartbeat goes to the same request. `AgentOutput.budget_exhausted` reports that the budget was spent (also set by `LLMAgentBlock`'s `max_iterations`/`max_tool_calls` exits), independently of `final_text`, so a host reads the cause without parsing `termination_reason`.
- **A run that ends without an answer is delivered, and labelled by cause** (`agent_stdin.py: _mark_turn_without_answer`): a run can still end with only what the model said on the way — "I will inspect now." and nothing else — when the answer request above returns nothing, or when the idle allowance runs out. Persisting that as the assistant's reply hands the user work nobody did: the same false completion the idle allowance exists to prevent, arriving through the other exit, and the host was checking only whether the response was *empty*. The block already reports the difference (`final_text` empty while `narration` is not), so the turn is now delivered with its text intact — dropping it would be the original defect again — followed by a notice that it is progress, not a finished reply. The notice itself is a stable, unlocalised marker (`TURN_CUT_SHORT_MARKER`), not translated prose, for the same reason `[INTERRUPTED]` is: the string is persisted and the front-end matches it to render the localised explanation and offer the **same continue action an interrupted turn offers** — a turn stopped mid-work is the same situation, and reusing that affordance keeps one concept instead of two. Persisting the translation would break the match for anyone who changed language after the turn ran. **The marker follows the cause, because the remedies differ**: `budget_exhausted` gets `TURN_CUT_SHORT_MARKER`, fixed by a larger budget; a run that stopped with steps left gets `TURN_NO_ANSWER_MARKER`, whose notice says a larger budget would not have helped and that the model may not be following the tool-calling protocol. Labelling both "ran out of steps" sent users after a limit the turn never reached. The cut-short notice offers both remedies: the button continues *this* turn, and the text names the budget for the *next* one — **the composer's "Effort / Budget" selector first**, and `Max Heartbeats (MemGPT)` in Project Settings as what its "Custom" option uses. The selector sends `max_heartbeats` with every run and `handle_run` gives it precedence, so outside "Custom" the project field changes nothing; the notice used to name that field alone. Every label is interpolated from the i18n catalogue rather than retyped, so the notice cannot go on pointing at a control that was renamed. The button is deliberately not labelled "give it more heartbeats" or "give it more time": it does not change the setting, and the guardrail counts model calls rather than seconds, so both would name something the control does not do. The `opalatex-progress` fence is namespaced for a related reason: a bare ```` ```progress ```` block is something a model can legitimately write when asked for one, and the stripper that keeps the markup out of the model's context would have eaten it.
- **The step budget warns before it runs out** (`memgpt_agent.py: heartbeat_pressure_threshold` / `_heartbeat_pressure_alert`, default `0.75`, overridable through `model_params`): the notice above repairs a cut-short turn after the fact, and the user pays for it — they read work in progress and press continue. The only signal the model had while there was still budget to react was the `[System: You have N heartbeats remaining.]` tail appended to each tool result: accurate bookkeeping that says nothing about what to do with the number, and easy to skim past at the end of a long result. Past the threshold, remaining budget stops being a footnote — every request now opens with a transient `SYSTEM ALERT: Heartbeat Pressure` naming the steps left and asking for brevity: keep text short, drop optional checks and exploration, finish what is in progress and deliver the answer while there are still steps to write it. It is deliberately modelled on `memory_pressure_threshold`, the same mechanism for the turn's other finite resource, down to being appended to the assembled request rather than to `internal_history` — a warning that accumulated would leave the model reading a pile of stale counts, each one wrong. It does not displace either terminal branch: `force_final_answer` and the exhausted-budget alert own the last request, and asking for brevity there would compete with asking for the answer. This is advice to the model, not a guarantee: a model that ignores it still spends `max_heartbeats`, is then asked for its answer with tool calls off, and gets the cut-short notice only if it gives none, which is why all three exist.
- **`create_plan` is plan mode's tool, and an approved plan is conversation** (`memgpt_runtime.py` tool composition, `tools.py: _persist_approved_plan`): the tool halts the turn on an approval dialog, which only means something where the mode is *propose, then execute*. Its description had always said "in plan mode", and that was not enough — observed with `openrouter/openai/gpt-5.6-luna` in an `auto` project asked only to *show* a correction plan: it opened an approval dialog, and `auto`'s own contract is that action is pre-authorized precisely so no dialog appears. It is now withheld by composing the orchestrator's tool list, the same mechanism and for the same reason as the delegate policy below: the shared mode gate in `opalatex_tool` wraps the very function object the worker calls. **The second half is the deliverable, and it is the pointer-shaped answer one layer up.** The plan reaches the user through a transient `input_request`, which is gone once they answer; that turn presented 5 817 characters and persisted the model's 857-character summary of them, so reopening the chat left a summary of a plan nobody could read any more. The agent's copy was never the gap — `create_plan` already returns the whole plan in its tool result — so the fix cannot be to ask the model to repeat it: relying on a model to re-deliver is exactly what failed in the entry above. An approved plan is therefore persisted by the host into `project_history`. It goes there rather than to `project_activity` because it is not an audit entry like the approval *decision* (PROJECT_DESIGN 2.5) but model-authored text addressed to the user, which the model then reads back like any other answer it gave. Only an approved plan is kept: replaying a rejected one would teach the model that the answer the user turned down was acceptable.
- **Agent turn checkpoint contract**: Every participating agent run creates a shadow-git start checkpoint before execution and finalizes an end checkpoint in `finally`-style cleanup, regardless of normal completion, failure, or thrown error. If the start and end checkpoints have no net diff, both checkpoints are discarded. If there is a net diff, the Review UI groups the matching `Agent turn start checkpoint[: label]` and `Agent turn end checkpoint[: label]` commits as one agent checkpoint row, using matching labels such as `worker:command-line` for worker turns, and the row diff compares start-to-end. **The contract has one blind spot, and it is reported rather than left silent**: a sub-directory of the project that carries its own `.git` is recorded by the shadow repository as a gitlink (mode `160000`), never as a tree of files — `git add .` stages a new commit id only when that repository's HEAD moves, and edits to the files inside it stage nothing at all. A turn that only touches those files therefore has no net diff, both checkpoints are discarded, and the Review UI shows nothing for work that really happened. `vcs.find_nested_git_repos` scans the project (bounded depth, skipping `node_modules`/virtualenvs/`.opalatex`) and `/api/git/nested-repos` serves the result: `App.jsx` logs a warning once per project when it is opened, and the Git sidebar shows a banner listing the folders whenever the agent history is the selected source. The fix is the user's to make — open the inner folder as the project, or remove its inner `.git` — so the app states the gap instead of working around it.
- **Checkpoint cleanup outlives cancellation, without delaying its acknowledgement** (`vcs.AsyncAgentTurnCheckpoint`, `agent_stdin.py`, `ide_server.py`): the interrupt endpoint still emits `cancelled` and returns immediately, so the button and chat react without waiting for Git. The event stream itself remains open until the turn task publishes its terminal sentinel, which prevents the front-end from starting a replacement turn while the old checkpoint is still closing; messages typed during that short interval fall through the existing backlog path and become the next turn. The server marks a cancellation request and calls `Task.cancel()` only once — `Task.cancelled()` cannot be used as the guard because it becomes true only after cleanup has already ended. Start and end Git operations run in their own shielded tasks and their caller absorbs any additional `CancelledError` until the operation's result is known, then propagates cancellation after the rest of the turn cleanup. This matters at both edges: cancellation can arrive while `begin_agent_turn_checkpoint` is still committing (the worker thread otherwise creates a start commit after its awaiter has forgotten the id), or while the end commit is being written. The inbox therefore opens only after the start snapshot is safely owned. A completed finalization emits `checkpoint_finalized`, which refreshes an already-open Review panel; a failed finalization emits a visible problem instead of being swallowed. The same owner wraps labeled `run_skill` worker checkpoints.
- **Two file markers, never conflated: ` *` is unsaved, the robot is the agent's change** (`utils/fileMarkers.js`, `components/AgentChangeBadge.jsx`, `FileNode.jsx`, `EditorPanel.jsx` tabs): ` *` means only what it means in every editor — the open buffer differs from the content last read from or written to disk (`diskFileContentsRef`), the same test the project-switch prompt uses. It used to compare with `originalFileContents`, which is the **git HEAD** baseline of the diff view: a saved file stayed starred until committed, every file of a project without a repository was starred, and an agent write starred its file only because the cache invalidation left `undefined` to compare with HEAD — so the star read as "the agent changed this" by accident. Files an agent turn changed get their own badge instead (lucide `Bot`, the symbol the Git sidebar uses for the agent history; folders show a fainter one when something inside is marked). The source is the turn checkpoint, not the tool calls: `finalize_agent_turn_checkpoint_details` lists, under the same lock as the end commit, the paths that differ between start and end (`--no-renames --diff-filter=d`; deletions have nothing to mark), and `checkpoint_finalized` carries them as `changed_files` plus `project_path`. Tool-name sniffing was rejected because `run_command`, scripts and image generation also write files. The GUI adds them only when `project_path` is the project on screen (read through `documentContextRef`, since the stream reader holds an old closure), clears a mark when the user opens the file, re-keys marks on rename/move, drops them on delete, and resets them on project switch or checkpoint restore. Known limits, inherited from the checkpoint: a user save made *during* a turn is attributed to the turn, files inside nested git repositories are never reported, and with git strategy `none` there is no checkpoint and therefore no badge. **Open buffers follow the agent's writes without losing the user's** (`utils/agentFileSync.js`): after each write-tool result (every path the turn's write tools named — tool events carry no call id to pair a result with its call), at `checkpoint_finalized` (its `changed_files`, which also covers workers, whose tools emit no tool events, and `run_command`) and at `agent_finished` (the only hook when git strategy is `none`), each buffered file the paths name — matched by project-relative path, never by basename, which used to reload a same-named file in another folder — is compared three ways: disk equal to its baseline → nothing; buffer equal to the baseline (or to the disk) → take the disk version; otherwise the buffer has unsaved edits and the user is asked (`showConfirm` with `destructive`, so Cancel/Escape/Enter keep the edits and only an explicit click discards them). The baseline follows the disk in both cases, so kept edits stay marked ` *` and the same write is not asked about twice. Questions are serialized, because `CustomDialogProvider` has one slot; opening a dialog over another now settles the first as dismissed instead of leaving its caller awaiting forever.
- **One agent turn at a time, and a turn outlives its client stream** (`ide_server.py` `/api/opalatex/run`, `agent_stdin._install_turn_input_hooks`): a turn keeps its project, store and GUI input hooks in module globals, so two live turns corrupt each other. Observed after a system standby dropped the GUI stream of a turn parked on `ask_question` (the embedded browser aborts in-flight requests when the system suspends): the GUI started a new turn, the parked turn was cancelled a few seconds later — its stream failure was only detected when the *new* turn's events were written to it — and its cleanup reset the new turn's hooks, so the new turn's question went to `input()` on the server console and never reached the GUI. Reproduced against a live socket on every revision from 17/08 on. Three rules close it. `/run` answers `409` (`reason: turn_active` or `turn_stopping`) while the previous turn's task is alive, and the front-end (`utils/agentRunConflict.js`) takes the unsent message back and *offers* to interrupt that turn rather than doing so on the user's behalf; this includes inline-editor runs, which previously overwrote a running chat turn's slot. **A lost stream never cancels the turn**: only an explicit interrupt (or server shutdown) does. A turn waiting on a question stays waiting however long the user takes, and the answer still reaches it through `/api/opalatex/input_response`, which does not depend on the stream; a turn that keeps working without a client persists its activity and final answer as usual and releases the active slot when its task ends (a done-callback, not the stream handler). An intermediate attempt that cancelled the turn as soon as the socket reached EOF turned every standby into a lost turn — the question window stayed on screen and its answer was refused with `No pending request with that id` — and was removed. And a turn removes a GUI input hook only while that hook is still its own, so a late cleanup can never unhook a turn it does not own. **Nothing else may rebind a running turn to another chat** (`ProjectData.apply_settings_from`, `AsyncHTTPServer._refuse_during_agent_turn`): the turn persists its reply, its activity and its working memory through `agent_stdin.current_project`, resolved at write time. `/api/opalatex/update-project` loads the project's *default* chat and used to assign it there; saving a setting while a turn ran in another chat moved that turn — traced in a real database, a "Continue" pressed in a secondary chat wrote its reply and all 745 activity rows into the default chat (then the "Main Chat", §2.8) and overwrote that chat's saved `agent_state`. Edited settings are now copied onto the live project (`PROJECT_SETTINGS_FIELDS`, never the chat id, history or per-chat core memory), so the turn keeps its chat and its end-of-turn save does not revert the edit; the orchestrator and tool context are rebuilt only between turns. Slash commands, which reload the project for the chat they name, answer `409` while a turn is alive, like `/run`.
- **The machine does not idle-sleep through an agent turn** (`opalatex/keep_awake.py`, acquired where `/api/opalatex/run` creates the turn task and released in its `release_turn` done-callback): the bullet above makes a standby *survivable*, which is not the same as acceptable. An agent turn is unattended work — the user starts it and looks away, which is exactly what the idle-sleep timer is written to detect — and a suspend kills every socket, so the window comes back having lost the stream of a turn the server still owns and the user is met with a dialog offering to kill the work they were waiting for. Reported from a real session as `Streaming error: Connection lost`. The hold is taken for the *turn*, not for the HTTP stream that started it, matching who owns the active slot: a turn whose client went away keeps the machine awake until it finishes. It asks only for **system** sleep to be held off — the display may still go dark, and a sleep the user asks for (lid, menu) is never blocked, which is the behaviour a long compile already has. Backends are per platform (`SetThreadExecutionState` with `ES_CONTINUOUS|ES_SYSTEM_REQUIRED` on Windows, held by a dedicated thread because the flag dies with the thread that set it; `caffeinate -i` on macOS; `systemd-inhibit --what=idle:sleep` on Linux), the hold is reference counted so a nested holder cannot release the turn's, and every failure is non-fatal and reported once: a machine with no inhibitor available still runs its turns, it just sleeps as it did before. A refused turn (`409`) takes no hold, since nothing would release it.
- **A turn that dies still leaves its work in the conversation** (`agent_stdin.py`: `_persist_unfinished_turn`, `TURN_FAILED_MARKER`, `_ACTIVE_VISIBLE_CHUNKS`): the chat and its history are not allowed to lose what the agent produced, for any reason. Two paths broke that rule. The provider-error handler persisted **nothing** — reported from a real session where a standby killed the connection mid-answer (`Streaming error: Connection lost`, then `Could not connect to ollama/glm-5.3-flash:cloud`): inspecting the store afterwards, that chat held exactly one row, the user's question, while the streamed answer survived only as `project_activity` fragments. Reopening it showed an empty-looking exchange, and "Continue" had no assistant turn to resume from, which is what the user saw as the chat clearing itself. The interrupted path had the same defect in a smaller shape: it stored `INTERRUPTED_AGENT_HISTORY_MARKER` *alone*, dropping the partial answer that was on screen. Both now go through one helper that stores the visible text the turn streamed, followed by its marker — the shape the cut-short path already used, so there is one convention rather than three. **A failure writes only when there is work to keep**: a turn that died having written nothing has lost nothing, and a bare marker would put a failure row into the conversation the model reads back — the very reason errors are panel activity rather than history (§2.5) — besides inventing a reply for a run that produced none, which §2.7 forbids. An interruption still records itself unconditionally, because the user stopping the agent is context the model needs. The text is accumulated at the single choke point every visible chunk passes through (`print_event`), because a turn that dies never reaches the code that would have composed its answer. The front-end matches `TURN_FAILED_MARKER` the way it matches the other two: the marker is split off, the localised notice (`app.turnFailedNotice`) replaces it, and the **same** continue action is offered — a turn stopped by a dead provider is the same situation for the user as one stopped by the guardrail. **The front-end had a matching loss of its own**: `handleSendMessage` cleared the live stream buffers *before* posting, so a run the backend then refused with `409` (exactly what a turn surviving a standby causes) destroyed the partial answer still on screen, which no longer existed anywhere else. Both send paths now clear those buffers only once the turn has been accepted.
- **The marker is a suffix, and one branch was still reading it as the whole message** (`gui_src/src/utils/turnMarkers.js`, `ChatPanel.jsx`, `App.jsx` `cancelled`): `_persist_unfinished_turn` stores `f"{visible}

{marker}"`, so the partial answer sits *above* the marker — that is the whole point of the entry above. The cut-short and failed branches were written against that shape and matched with `includes`; the interruption branch kept its older test, `content === marker`, which is only ever true for a turn that produced nothing. **Interrupting an agent while it was talking therefore lost its continue action and printed the raw English marker into the chat as if the model had written it** ("[INTERRUPTED] The user interrupted the agent execution." under the sentence the agent had started), reported from a real session. The three markers now live in one module and are read by one function, `turnEndFromContent`, which reports how the turn ended and returns the text without the markup; the notice is rendered beside the continue button rather than *replacing* the content, because replacing it would drop the partial answer this protocol exists to keep. The live path had the matching half of the bug: on `cancelled` it cleared the streamed text and appended a marker-only bubble, so the screen and the store told two different stories about one event until the chat was reloaded — it now writes the same shape the backend persists (`interruptedTurnContent`). Turns recorded before the marker existed (`Interrupted:` / `Interrompido:` prose) are still recognised and still replaced by the localised notice, since they have no work underneath to preserve. `tests/test_turn_protocol.py` asserts the marker parity *and* that no second copy of the string is left in `ChatPanel.jsx`; `gui_src/src/utils/__tests__/turnMarkers.test.js` covers the mid-sentence interruption that started this.
- **Common event types**: Agent runs may emit `agent_started`, `thought`, `reflection`, `stream_chunk`, `stream_retract`, `agent_step`, `tool_call`, `tool_result`, `problem`, `input_request`, `user_message_saved`, `token_usage`, `provider_wait`, `agent_response`, `cancelled`, `checkpoint_finalized`, `ide_command`, `latex_compiled`, and `agent_finished`. The front-end treats these as UI state transitions rather than opaque text. `agent_step` carries `{ step: int, max_steps: int | None, agent: str }` to report the number of executed heartbeats/steps in the turn so the UI displays live progress.
- **A silent provider is reported, never acted on** (`agenticblocks/blocks/llm/response_wait.py`, `on_response_wait` / `response_wait_notice_after` on both agent blocks, `provider_wait` event): observed in a real session, a pooled connection to the Ollama cloud died while the next request was being sent — the socket held unacknowledged data with its retransmission timer at the 120 s maximum. From the client that is indistinguishable from a model still thinking, so the turn sat on the 600 s HTTP timeout with only "Analyzing the obtained result..." on screen; LiteLLM's Router then retried on a fresh connection and the answer arrived in about a second. Each LLM call now runs a watch that, after 60 s without any data from the provider (before the first chunk, or between chunks), calls `on_response_wait` with `waiting=True`, and again with `waiting=False` when data resumes or the call ends. OpalaTex publishes it as `provider_wait` for the orchestrator and for skill workers; the desktop chat shows a notice in the running bubble with a locally ticking counter, and the CLI prints one line, both suggesting *interrupt, then Continue* (which closes the dead connection and resumes from the saved working memory). **It changes nothing about the request**: no shorter timeout, no extra retry, no duplicate request. Those were weighed against the measured first-token latency of cloud models (median 1.5 s, but legitimate waits up to 324 s on `glm-5.3:cloud`) and rejected for now — a short timeout would cut real answers, and a hedged second request costs tokens. A separate inter-chunk stall timeout (p99.9 gap ≤ 17 s measured) remains a possible future step.
- **Client-selectable heartbeat effort modes**: In addition to the project-level `max_heartbeats` setting, the chat composer offers an expandable effort selector (similar to modern AI IDEs) with four modes: Low (20 heartbeats), Medium (50 heartbeats, default), High (100 heartbeats), and Custom (project settings). The chosen budget is passed via `max_heartbeats` in the `/api/opalatex/run` payload and dynamically overrides `agent.max_heartbeats` for that run without altering stored project settings. Omitting `max_heartbeats` or selecting Custom restores the project's configured value.
- **`think` is the provider's parsing switch, never a display switch** (`config.resolve_think_request`): Ollama's `think: false` does not stop a reasoning model from reasoning — it stops Ollama from separating the reasoning into its own `thinking` field. Probed against `ollama_chat/glm-5.3:cloud`: with `think: false` the answer came back as `"17*23 = 17*20 + 17*3 = 340 + 51 = 391"` followed by `"391"` in `content`, `reasoning_content` empty; with `think: true`, `content` was `"391"` and the working went to `reasoning_content`. In the off case the reasoning arrives **undelimited** — no `<think>` tags at all in a 143k-character stream, nothing any text splitter can key on — and was published as the assistant's answer. So a model whose catalog entry declares `supports_thinking` is **always** sent `think: true`, and that same resolved flag is what gates `on_thinking`: the reasoning is published exactly when the provider was asked to isolate it. There is no second switch — see *Thinking is a model capability, and only a model capability* below. This is the same separation `resolve_model_route` makes for transport — a preference must not silently degrade the wire format.
