Rather than instrument each of those, `ide_server.py` announces "this project changed" at a few choke points — the file endpoints, and the end of an agent turn, which covers every tool that turn used including an interrupted one — and `CloudSyncManager` runs a pass once the project has been quiet for a debounce interval, plus a slow poll to pick up another machine's edits. Passes run in a worker thread and are serialized per project; concurrent passes would race on the baseline.
- **OAuth is forced into the system browser** (`cloud/oauth.py`): the UI is an embedded webview, and Google refuses to serve its consent screen to embedded user agents. The flow is therefore Authorization Code + PKCE with a loopback redirect on an ephemeral port. Only the **`drive.file`** scope is requested — per-file access to what the app creates — which Google classifies as non-sensitive and which therefore needs neither the verification review nor the annual third-party (CASA) security assessment that the full `drive` scope triggers. That distinction is a recurring cost decision, not a detail: the restricted scope would attach a paid annual audit to an MIT project with no revenue.
- **The OAuth client ships with the build, so connecting is one click**: asking every user to register their own Google Cloud project put a developer chore in front of a consumer feature — the Account tab opened on a client id field, and nobody who was not already a Google Cloud user got past it. A release therefore carries a "Desktop app" client injected at build time by `scripts/embed_google_client.py` into `opalatex/cloud/providers/bundled_google_client.json` (git-ignored; the values come from the release pipeline's secrets, `OPALATEX_GDRIVE_CLIENT_ID`/`_SECRET`), and the packaged app reads it as package data. That the secret travels in the binary is not the leak it looks like: Google classifies desktop clients as *public* clients, which is exactly why the flow is PKCE-bound. What it does cost is real and accepted — every user's Drive traffic sits on one project's quota, and the consent screen names the maintainer, who must publish it (with only `drive.file`, publishing needs no verification review). `describe_client_config()` resolves the effective client as environment → user override → bundled, so a user who wants their own quota and consent screen can still register a client under "Use my own Google OAuth client" and it wins; a build with no bundled client falls back to that form instead of failing. Refresh tokens and any user-supplied secret live in `~/.opalatex/cloud/` written `0600`, with the mode set on the temp file before the atomic replace so the content is never briefly world-readable.
- **The Drive backend is stdlib-only**: `google-api-python-client` would pull a large transitive tree into a packaged desktop build and its dynamic discovery is a recurring PyInstaller failure, for what amounts to five REST endpoints. Deletes go to the Drive trash rather than being permanent — a bug in reconciliation must not be able to destroy the user's only copy. A file's revision is its content checksum where Drive supplies one, because Drive's `version` counter also increments on metadata changes and would make a rename look like an edit on every other machine.
- **Connections race the resolved addresses instead of walking them** (`cloud/transport.py`): staying on the standard library meant inheriting `socket.create_connection`, which tries each address in turn and spends the *whole* socket timeout on every one that does not answer. The Drive backend's timeout is necessarily long because it streams large files, so on a connection with partially broken IPv6 routing to Google — six of the eight `www.googleapis.com` AAAA records blackholing while every A record answered in 40 ms — the project listing hung for minutes and surfaced as a spinner that never resolved. Nothing in the app could report it, because no request had failed yet. Browsers and other desktop sync clients on the same machine were unaffected, which is what made it read as an application bug rather than a network one: they implement RFC 8305 (Happy Eyeballs), and `urllib` does not. The cloud package now connects through its own opener that interleaves the address families, starts an attempt every 250 ms, takes the first socket to complete and closes the losers. It also separates the two timeouts that `create_connection` conflates: the handshake is bounded at 10 s so a silent address is abandoned quickly, while reads and writes keep the caller's generous timeout so a 100 MB download is not cut short. Measured against the reporting account, listing went from never completing to 1.0 s. `oauth.py` routes through it too — the token endpoint is the same infrastructure, and a stalled refresh is a hung sync.

- **Exclusion applies to the entire reconciliation**: the same policy filters local, remote and baseline paths before planning transfers or deletions. A skipped file is not a deleted file: excluded paths retain their baseline and both copies, so removing an exclusion later reconciles against the last agreed version. Local size/symlink skips are also honored. Machine-local sync state, checkpoint repositories, nested dependency/cache directories and oversized remote files are excluded. `.env` requires explicit opt-in; disabling chat sync also excludes its existing export. Ignoring a path never means deleting its remote copy.
- **An upload sends a stable snapshot**: normal uploads, precondition retries and conflict uploads copy the source into a temporary directory outside the project, reject a source that changes while the copy is prepared, and upload those bytes. The baseline records the snapshot's hash and the source metadata observed when it was copied, never metadata sampled after the upload. Typing while the upload is in flight therefore invalidates the scanner's cache on the next pass. Temporary snapshots are removed on success and failure.
- **The user can see what is happening, per file**: a pass used to run behind a spinner that said only "Syncing…", which cannot distinguish a working sync from a stuck one, and nothing said which files were already in the cloud. Three things now report it. (1) `engine.py` emits a typed `ProgressEvent` for every step — a `plan` event carrying the total, then one per file, including the ones that need no transfer, so the counter keeps moving through a long stretch of unchanged files. (2) `service.py` folds those into a `SyncProgress` snapshot per project, guarded by a lock because the pass runs in a worker thread while the request handler reads it from the event loop; the snapshot outlives the pass, so the panel keeps showing what the last one moved. (3) `file_states()` compares the working copy against the baseline and answers `synced` / `pending` / `syncing` / `conflict` / `excluded` per path, with directories inheriting the worst state below them so a collapsed folder still shows that something inside needs attention. `FileNode.jsx` renders it as a badge the way a desktop Drive client does. The scan behind it is memoized for a few seconds and invalidated at the end of a pass, because the front-end polls every second while one runs — a badge must not cost more than the sync it describes.
- **A pass can be stopped, but only between files**: a sync over a large project, or the download of one, runs for minutes, and neither used to offer a way out — the download modal even disabled Close. `service.request_cancel(path)` (`POST /api/cloud/cancel`) sets a flag on that path's `SyncProgress` snapshot, which every kind of pass already registers under the path it writes to (a manual or background sync, a preview, a download into its destination), so one mechanism covers all of them. The engine polls it through `should_cancel` before listing and before each file, never mid-transfer: the file in flight finishes, so the baseline never records a file that did not fully arrive and does record every file that did, and a cancelled sync is simply a partial pass the next one continues (`report.cancelled`, conversation merge skipped). A cancelled **download** differs from a failed one: nobody needs to inspect what arrived, so `clone_project` removes it — safe because the destination was verified empty before the first byte, so everything in it came from the clone and is still in the cloud — and removes the directory itself only when the clone created it. The endpoint answers such a download with `200 {"cancelled": true}` and registers nothing. Interrupting inside a single large transfer would need the provider facade to accept a cancel token; it does not yet.
- **A vanished remote folder stops the pass; it is never re-created silently**: both providers used to answer "the recorded root is gone" by creating a new, empty folder. The baseline then read every tracked file as deleted in the cloud and deleted the working copy — the bulk-delete guard only intervenes at ten or more files, so a small project on another computer was wiped outright (verified against `local_folder`: both files of a two-file project deleted). `ensure_root` now raises `CloudRootMissing` when an `existing_root` no longer exists (Drive: a 404 or a trashed folder; a network failure propagates as itself so it is never read as a deletion), and the engine reports `remote_missing` with nothing touched on either side. Only the user can say what that means, so the sync panel offers *Upload again as a new copy* (reset the baseline, then sync) or *Turn off cloud sync*.
- **Deleting a project can delete its cloud copy too**: the delete dialog asks `/api/cloud/link` (local state only, no network) whether the project has a cloud folder — one a pass has recorded — and then offers the option, off by default, naming the provider and folder and saying whether it is recoverable (`Capabilities.recoverable_root_deletion`). `CloudStorageProvider.delete_root` is optional in the facade: Drive moves the folder to the trash, consistent with every other Drive delete; `local_folder` has no trash, removes it permanently, and refuses anything outside its base directory. `CloudSyncManager.delete_remote_copy` cancels any running pass and takes the project's lock before deleting, so no pass writes into a folder being removed. It runs *before* any local deletion and can veto it: if the cloud copy cannot be deleted, nothing is, and the project stays in place for a retry. Other computers mirroring the project stop at the missing-root check above with their files intact. Deleting a project, with or without this option, now also stops its background sync task, which used to outlive the project.
- **A conflict is a question, not a verdict**: the pass keeps the working copy, uploads it, and parks the other machine's version beside it, which loses nothing but leaves the user with two files and no way to say which wins. `service.resolve_conflict` applies the answer on both sides — `keep_local` re-sends the working copy, `keep_remote` promotes the conflict copy over it and uploads that. Note where the cloud's version actually is at that point: the conflicted pass already sent the local one, so `keep_remote` reads the conflict copy rather than downloading, and *fails* when that copy is missing rather than silently handing back the version the user just rejected. Either way the baseline is rewritten to the resolved content, so the next pass sees one agreed version. "Keep both" needs no backend call — it is the state a conflict already leaves behind.
- **Getting the project onto the second machine is its own flow, not a sync**: mirroring is only half of "work on this from another computer" — the other machine has no project to enable sync *for*. `service.clone_project` answers that: it enumerates the account (`CloudStorageProvider.list_projects`, guarded by the new `Capabilities.project_listing` so a backend addressing a single folder can decline rather than answer "empty"), writes the project's cloud settings, and runs the engine in the **PULL** direction into a folder that must be new or empty. Empty is enforced, not preferred: a pull into a directory that already holds files reconciles them against the remote, which turns "download a copy" into a sync with whatever happened to be there. The listing carries the provider's own root handle alongside the name, so the clone addresses the folder that was listed instead of resolving the name a second time — on Drive, resolving by name is what creates a *second* folder with that name. Registering the project (its `sessions.db` row, its conversations) stays with the endpoint: the cloud package knows storage, not the project store.
- **Registration runs after the download, and must not scaffold over it**: `ProjectStore.create` writes a starter `skills.yaml`, so registering first would overwrite the one that just arrived — and the next pass would publish that loss back to the machine the project came from. Hence `create(preserve_existing_skills=True)`, used only by this path. The order also matters for the conversations: `chats.merge_into_database` refuses to write history for a project that has no row yet, so the merge is the last step. It runs with `adopt_settings=True`, the one place that bypass is correct — the row was created seconds ago, so plain last-writer-wins would always rank the empty local settings above the ones that were synced, and the clone would arrive with no model, mode or main file. The clone also reuses the *internal* project name from the incoming export when that name is free here, because chat ids are derived from it (`main_<name>`); a fresh key would leave the clone with an empty default chat beside the imported one.
- **A failed download is not registered and not resumed**: whatever arrived stays on disk to be inspected, which leaves the folder non-empty, so the error says to remove it or pick another name rather than letting the retry fail on "not empty" with no explanation. Resuming would mean reconciling a half-copy against the remote, which is the case the empty-folder rule exists to prevent.
- **PULL now honours its own contract.** `SyncEngine._resolve_conflict` used to upload the working copy regardless of direction, so a one-way fetch could overwrite the very version it was asked to fetch. In PULL it now parks the remote version beside the local one, reports the conflict and leaves the baseline alone for a later two-way pass to settle. Nothing else used PULL before; the clone does.
- **UI**: `gui_src/src/components/modals/CloudSyncModal.jsx`, reached from the activity bar, with a status indicator in the footer that names the file in flight and a per-file badge in the workspace tree. Downloading a project is `gui_src/src/components/modals/CloudDownloadModal.jsx`, reached from the cloud icon in the Explorer header beside New and Import — deliberately not a tab of the sync panel, which requires the project the flow exists to go and fetch. Connecting there is the **same one-click browser authorization** the sync panel uses and nothing else: the OAuth client ships with the build (§ above), so the flow is Connect → authorize → pick a project. The authorization URL both panels print as a fallback is a link that opens through `/api/system/open-url`, not a bare `target="_blank"`: the desktop shell is a pywebview window with no `createWindow` handler, where a blank-target click does nothing at all and leaves the user staring at a URL they cannot follow. The endpoint accepts only `http`/`https`, so it cannot be used to hand an arbitrary scheme to the desktop opener. It deliberately carries no client-id form — a second machine is precisely where a user is least willing to go and register credentials in a cloud console, and a build that somehow ships no client is a build problem, answered by the existing message from `begin_authorization` rather than by a second copy of the Account tab's form. Endpoints are `/api/cloud/*` in `ide_server.py`; the download flow adds `/api/cloud/auth-status` (project-less connection check), `/api/cloud/remote-projects` (listing, annotated with any local project already mirroring that folder) and `/api/cloud/clone`; `/api/cloud/cancel` stops a running sync or download for a path; `/api/cloud/link` tells the delete dialog whether a project has a cloud copy, and `/api/opalatex/delete` accepts `delete_cloud`.
- **Tests**: the sync engine's contract suite (`tests/test_cloud_sync.py`) runs against `providers/local_folder.py`, a real provider implementation rather than a mock, so conflict handling, exclusions and the bulk-delete guard are exercised offline and without credentials. That provider is also useful on its own, pointed at a mounted share or an rclone mount. `tests/test_cloud_clone.py` covers the download half against the same provider, including the endpoints themselves — a project pushed from one machine's database and store, then cloned into a second machine's, is the scenario the feature exists for.

- **A sync pass does not rebuild an unchanged export, and never serializes it in one call** (`project._install_history_revisions`, `chats.refresh_export` / `write_export`): the export used to be rebuilt from every row and compared byte for byte on every pass. Measured on a project with 950k activity rows: 8.5 s to build, and a single `json.dumps` of the 309 MB document — which since Python 3.14 runs entirely in the C encoder even with `indent` — held the interpreter for ~4 s, freezing the server, the integrated terminal and the window together on every pass, to find the bytes unchanged. Two changes answer it. (1) Triggers on `projects`, `project_chats`, `project_history` and `project_activity` keep a per-project counter (`project_revisions`) that every insert, update and delete moves — the store's write paths, the merge's own connection and an older build alike. `project_export_marks` records, per export file, the counter it was built at and the file's identity (size, mtime, inode, format version). A pass whose counter and file both match skips the export outright (measured: 0.00 s instead of ~13 s); the counter is read *before* the rows, so a write landing mid-export leaves the mark behind the data and the next pass rebuilds. Every UPDATE counts, even of a column the export does not carry: a false "changed" costs one rebuild, a missed change would leave the export stale. The trigger bodies avoid UPSERT so any SQLite that reads the schema can run them. Cost: ~10 µs per stored row, and deleting a large chat takes about twice as long (0.58 s → 1.24 s for 320k rows). (2) When it is rebuilt, `_serialized_chunks` produces exactly `json.dumps(indent=2, ensure_ascii=False, sort_keys=True)` through `JSONEncoder.iterencode` in ~64 KB pieces, compared with the current file while written to a temp file, so neither document is held whole. On the same project the longest stall another thread sees during a rebuild fell from ~4 s to ~0.24 s — what remains is the C `list.sort` of the rows (~0.14 s) and the cyclic GC, both inside `export_project` — at about twice the total CPU, in the sync worker thread. The row order and the bytes are deliberately unchanged: two machines converge only if they produce identical bytes for the same union. Reading a downloaded export (`json.load` in `read_export_from`) still parses the whole document in one call.
- **Other devices write journals, never `chats.json`** (`cloud/state.py: DEVICE_JOURNALS_REL_DIR`, `chats.merge_device_journal`): the mobile app (§2.21) continues a project's conversations, and a second writer of the export would race the desktops that rewrite it after every merge. Each device appends to `.opalatex/session/devices/<id>.json`, which only it writes; a pass merges every journal it downloaded and republishes the union through the export, so other computers get those rows without reading journals at all. A journal may create chats (identity only) and add messages and activity; project settings and a chat's working state in it are ignored rather than applied. The same toggle that keeps the export home (`include_chats`) keeps journals home, and a clone merges journals present on disk after the export. Normative format: [docs/specs/chats_export.md](docs/specs/chats_export.md).
- **A turn merged from elsewhere reaches the orchestrator, not just the chat** (`project_chats.external_turns`, `memgpt_runtime._apply_external_turns`): the orchestrator restores its context from `agent_state`, which no merge touches, so a turn made on another machine or the phone used to show up in the chat and stay unknown to the agent answering the next message — a gap that already existed between two desktops. A merge that adds visible user/assistant messages to a chat that already had a saved `internal_history` appends them to a per-chat queue; the restore appends the queue after the restored history through agenticblocks' general `MemGPTAgentBlock.append_external_messages` (after the usual repair of an unanswered tool call), and the queue is consumed by count only when that state is saved, so a failed turn loses nothing and arrivals during a turn survive it. Chats that seed from history discard the queue; clearing or superseding a chat resets it with `agent_state`. It is a separate column because a pass merges in a worker thread while a turn may be saving `agent_state`. Covered by `tests/test_cloud_device_journals.py` and `tests/test_memgpt_external_messages.py`.
- **Atomic writes tolerate a short-lived Windows lock, and their temp files are never mirrored** (`cloud/atomic.py`, `scanner.ALWAYS_EXCLUDED`): every atomic write (conversation export, sync baseline, file downloads, credentials) replaces its target from a temp file in the same directory. On Windows another process — antivirus or the search indexer — often holds a freshly written file open for a moment, and the replace fails with a sharing violation. Observed on a 23 MB `chats.json` export: the replace failed, deleting the temp file failed for the same reason, and the next pass uploaded two stray `.chats-*.tmp` files (49 MB) as project content. `replace_file` / `discard_file` retry exactly that failure (`winerror` 5 or 32) for about three seconds and then raise the original error; any other error is raised at once. The temp-file patterns of these writes are excluded from the scan. Excluding a path does not delete a copy that was already mirrored (an excluded tracked file keeps both copies and its baseline), so such a stray is removed locally *before* the exclusion is in effect, letting a pass delete the remote copy. A pass that still fails outside the anticipated `CloudError`s is recorded in the outcome with its type by `CloudSyncManager._sync_blocking`, and the background loop keeps running; it used to die on an `UnboundLocalError` that hid the real error.

### 2.16 Accessibility: Interface Scale

- **One global scale, applied as a `zoom` on the app root**: `Settings > General > Interface Size` offers a named ladder (Small 90% / Medium 100% / Large 120% / Extra Large 140%) plus a slider reaching 80–200%, and `Ctrl+Shift+Plus` / `Ctrl+Shift+Minus` / `Ctrl+Shift+0` anywhere in the app. `.vscode-app` turns the factor into a CSS `zoom`, so text, icons, padding and click targets grow together instead of the text alone outgrowing the chrome around it. A text-only scale was rejected on purpose: hundreds of components size themselves with literal pixel values in inline styles, which no CSS variable can reach, so `zoom` is both the cheaper and the better-proportioned option. Per-panel zoom (editor font size, chat, PDF, Markdown preview, terminal) composes on top of it — the global scale sets the comfortable baseline, the panel controls tune one pane relative to it.
- **Viewport units are the one thing `zoom` does not compensate**: percentages and `width: auto` are resolved correctly at the zoom boundary by Chromium 128+, but `100vh` resolves against the real viewport and is *then* multiplied, overflowing the screen. `.vscode-app` therefore divides — `height: calc(100 * var(--ui-vh))` where `--ui-vh: calc(1vh / var(--ui-scale))` — and everything below it uses `--ui-vh` / `--ui-vw` instead of `vh` / `vw`. The division is correct under both the compensating and the older non-compensating implementation of `zoom`, so the layout holds on the Qt/Chromium and the WebKitGTK webview backend alike.
- **Pointer and rect coordinates must cross the zoom boundary explicitly**: DOM geometry — `MouseEvent.clientX/clientY`, `getBoundingClientRect()`, `window.innerWidth/innerHeight` — is reported in real viewport pixels, but a CSS length written into the zoomed subtree is multiplied by the scale before it is painted. A pointer position assigned straight to `left`/`top` therefore lands scale-times too far from the origin, which is how a context menu opened at the top of the file tree ended up near the bottom of the window at 140%. `utils/uiScale.js` exposes `viewportPointToApp` / `viewportPxToApp` for that conversion, and every overlay positioned from a pointer or a measured rect goes through it: the workspace, tab, text, editor and PDF context menus, the PDF tooltip, note and translation popups, the inline prompt overlay, panel resizing, and the SyncTeX click mapping. Convert *only* at the point where a measurement becomes a CSS length — hit-testing a pointer against a `getBoundingClientRect` compares two viewport values and must be left alone.
- **xterm gets a local 1:1 coordinate space without becoming visually smaller**: xterm 6 measures its cells in unscaled CSS pixels but derives selection and mouse-report offsets from the scaled `getBoundingClientRect()`. At 140%, a pointer over row six is consequently interpreted near row eight, with the error growing down the terminal. `.xterm-coordinate-space` cancels the app zoom around the xterm surface, while `terminalRenderFontSize` multiplies its configured font size by the same factor; the rendered glyphs, row count and per-terminal zoom therefore stay unchanged, but pointer offsets and cell dimensions now use the same unit. Both the bottom-panel terminal and the interactive approval terminal use this boundary. The browser suite performs a real drag at multiple interface scales and verifies the selected buffer row.
- **The PDF canvas gets the scale as device pixels, not layout**: pdf.js paints into a canvas, so a `zoom` over it would stretch a fixed bitmap and blur the page. `PdfPreview` folds the factor into react-pdf's `devicePixelRatio` instead, which renders the backing store at the size it is actually displayed at and leaves `scale` — and the zoom percentage in the toolbar — meaning what it meant before.
- **This setting lives in `ui_settings.json`, not localStorage**: unlike theme and editor font size, the interface scale is stored server-side (`/api/settings/appearance`, `ui_scale`) and restored on startup. It is the one setting a user with low vision cannot work around if it silently resets, so it belongs in the store whose stated purpose is surviving webview sessions. Both ends clamp it, and anything non-numeric falls back to 1.0 rather than to the minimum, so a corrupted file cannot render the app unreadable.
- **The floor was raised alongside it**: a relative scale multiplies whatever base it is given, so 9px text at "Medium" stays 9px. Every sub-11px size in the interface was raised to 11px, the one exception being the ActivityBar's notification count, a numeric badge inside a fixed 16px circle that would overflow (and which still grows with the global scale).
- **Fixed-height chrome has to give way, not clip**: the scale multiplies every button in the ActivityBar while the space between the top bar and the status bar stays the same, so at 140% in a laptop-sized window the strip ran past the bottom of the window and the last icons — settings, hardware — were simply gone. The bar now measures itself and steps down a ladder of density tiers (`gui_src/src/utils/activityBarDensity.js`), tightening gaps, padding and icon sizes together; the tiers are written into CSS custom properties and passed to lucide from that one table, so `index.css` cannot drift away from the fit calculation, and the button counts are read from the DOM so adding a button keeps the arithmetic honest. Below the densest tier the bar scrolls — with a fade on whichever edge still hides a button — because a strip that ends in a half-drawn icon reads as a rendering glitch, and a button that cannot be reached at all is a lost feature. Two triggers are needed, not one: a `ResizeObserver` for window resizes, and a `MutationObserver` on `<html>`'s inline style for the scale itself, since raising the scale shrinks the bar in its own CSS pixels while the box it paints keeps the same size on screen — a change `ResizeObserver` never reports.
- **Tests**: `tests/test_ui_scale.py` covers the clamp, persistence across a restart, and the endpoint; `gui_src/src/utils/__tests__/uiScale.test.js` covers the ladder and the rounding; `gui_src/src/utils/__tests__/activityBarDensity.test.js` covers the tier ladder, its monotonicity, and the fallbacks.

### 2.16.1 Native Crashes and the Window's Graphics Mode

- **A crash below Python leaves no traceback, so the application has to notice it afterwards** (`opalatex/crash_report.py`, `/api/diagnostics/startup-report`, `App.jsx`): QtWebEngine does not run its GPU service in a child process the way a stand-alone Chromium does — on Windows the graphics driver is loaded into the OpalaTex process itself, which the Windows Error Reporting record of the incident that prompted this confirms (`igd10um64xe.dll`, `igd10iumd64.dll`, `d3d11.dll`, `dxgi.dll` all loaded in the faulting process). A fault there is therefore a fault in OpalaTex: the window disappears, no Python exception is ever raised, and the terminal stays empty. Six such crashes were recorded in one day, every one an access violation at the same address inside `Qt6WebEngineCore.dll` 6.11.2, and the user's only evidence was that the app "closed by itself" while an agent turn was streaming reasoning at ~160 events a second. **Two mechanisms close that gap, and they are deliberately independent.** `faulthandler` writes the fatal signal and every thread's Python stack to `<OPALATEX_HOME>/logs/crash.log` as the process dies; because it installs a Windows *vectored* handler it also records survivable first-chance faults, so an entry in the log proves a fault happened, not that it was fatal — which is exactly why the log is not what decides whether the previous run died. A **run marker** decides that: every run writes `logs/runs/<pid>-<start>.json` and deletes it on the way out, so a marker still on disk whose process is gone is a run that never reached its exit path. The marker is refreshed on a heartbeat, which keeps a reused pid from masking a dead run and keeps a *second* live instance — the app allows one — from being reported as the first one's crash. Exit paths that bypass `atexit` (the GUI server and the restart timer both end in `os._exit`) call `mark_clean_exit` themselves; a missed call costs a spurious notice, never a missed one, which is the right direction for this trade. The notice reaches the user in the Output panel on the next launch, with the log path, and the marker is archived under `logs/runs/reported/` rather than deleted, so the evidence outlives the notice. Reading the report does not consume it: reloading the window must not erase the only notice a silent crash ever produces.
- **The graphics mode is a user decision, never a silent substitution** (`opalatex/webengine_env.py`, `Settings > Window graphics`, `ui_settings.json: webengine_gpu`): the one defence the application has against a driver fault is asking Chromium not to use the GPU, and that is a real cost in compositing smoothness — so it is offered, not applied behind the user's back. `auto` (the default) uses the GPU; `off` appends `--disable-gpu` to `QTWEBENGINE_CHROMIUM_FLAGS` **and** sets `QT_QUICK_BACKEND=software`. **The flag alone was not enough, and the first version shipped with only the flag**: it stops *Chromium* from using the GPU — WebGL disappears, and the GPU service logs `Failed to create GLES3 context` / `kFatalFailure: Failed to create shared context for virtualization`, which is the expected signature of the flag working, not a new fault — but Qt composites the web view into the window through its own scene graph, Direct3D 11 by default on Windows. An off-screen A/B on the reporting machine (Iris Xe, driver 31.0.101.4502) measured all four Intel driver DLLs (`igd10um64xe`, `igd10iumd64`, `igc64`, `igdgmm64`) still loaded with the flag; adding the software scene graph leaves only `igd10um64xe`, which DXGI loads when enumerating adapters, and every variant rendered the test canvas correctly. A `QT_QUICK_BACKEND` the user set by hand is kept, and `OPALATEX_DISABLE_GPU` overrides the saved value **in both directions**, so a user whose window dies before Settings can be opened still has a way in, and one who wants the GPU back for a single run does not have to change what is saved. Flags the user set are appended to, never replaced. QtWebEngine reads them once, before the browser exists, so the setting applies at the next launch and the UI offers the existing in-app restart rather than pretending otherwise. Every run records its own mode in the crash log banner, which is what makes "did turning it off help?" a question the logs can answer instead of a recollection. Linux keeps the default it already had — `--disable-gpu` unless the variable is set by hand — for the unrelated reason that Mesa's Zink/EGL layer can abort at startup when it cannot acquire a DRM render node.
- **A restart must not inherit the decision it is replacing** (`webengine_env.pristine_environment`, `ide_server.spawn_detached(env=...)`): the graphics mode is applied by writing environment variables, and the in-app restart spawns the replacement as a *child* of the process being replaced — so the child inherited them, and a setting that "applies at the next launch" did not apply at the next launch. Caught in the crash log rather than by reasoning: a run recorded `webengine_gpu: auto` in its own banner while carrying an inherited `chromium_flags: --disable-gpu`, which is the app reporting one thing and doing another, the single failure this log exists to make impossible. The child is now handed the environment as it was *before* this module wrote to it — restored, not deleted, so a `QTWEBENGINE_CHROMIUM_FLAGS` the user exported themselves survives a restart, while the flags pywebview appends afterwards do not accumulate one copy per restart.
- **Nor may it inherit a disabled Ctrl+C** (`ide_server.spawn_detached`, `terminal_manager.enable_ctrl_c_for_children`): on Windows the replacement is started with `DETACHED_PROCESS` only. It used to add `CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP`, which makes Windows disable Ctrl+C in the new process (an implicit `SetConsoleCtrlHandler(NULL, TRUE)`) and in everything it creates; ConPTY (pywinpty) spawns PowerShell without a process group of its own, so after an in-app restart the integrated terminal delivered `\x03` as a CTRL_C_EVENT that PowerShell and the running command ignored. A detached process has no console, so the group flag protected nothing. `TerminalSession` also calls `SetConsoleCtrlHandler(NULL, FALSE)` before spawning the shell, so the terminal honours Ctrl+C however OpalaTex itself was launched; a failure is printed, not hidden. Covered by `tests/test_windows_terminal_ctrl_c.py`.
- **The declared Qt floor is not a supported floor, and two downgrades proved it** (`requirements.txt`, `pyproject.toml`: `PyQt6>=6.8.0`, `PyQt6-WebEngine>=6.8.0`): testing whether the graphics crash was specific to Qt 6.11 meant installing older releases, and each broke the application in its own way before the GPU could be tested at all. **Qt 6.8.2** embeds Chromium 122, while pdf.js 5.4.296 (through `react-pdf` 10.4.1) calls `URL.parse`, which arrived in Chrome 126 — opening a PDF threw and the whole window went grey, while the same page worked in a current Chrome. **Qt 6.10.2** (Chromium 134, so PDFs render) ships `MSVCP140.dll` **14.26** in `PyQt6/Qt6/bin`, and `onnxruntime` 1.30 — loaded lazily by ChromaDB's embedding function on the first archival write, i.e. the first agent message — needs a newer C++ runtime and brings none of its own. Windows binds a DLL name once per process, so whichever runtime loads first serves everyone: the GUI starts first, Qt's 14.26 wins, and the import dies in `MSVCP140.dll`. Reproduced without a window in both orders (Qt then `onnxruntime`: segmentation fault; `onnxruntime` then Qt: works, on the system's 14.50). **Qt 6.11.2** ships 14.44 and passes both checks, which is why the application had been working. The observed truth is therefore narrower than the declaration: on Windows, 6.8 cannot view PDFs and 6.10.2 cannot run the agent. **The floor now declares what was verified**: `PyQt6>=6.11.0` and `PyQt6-WebEngine>=6.11.0`, plus `PyQt6-Qt6>=6.11.2` and `PyQt6-WebEngine-Qt6>=6.11.2` — the `-Qt6` packages are the ones that carry the C++ runtime, and PyQt6 itself only asks for `>=6.11.0`, which would admit patch releases whose runtime nobody checked. It lives in both `requirements.txt` and `pyproject.toml` (the snap installs from the latter), and `tests/test_dependency_floors.py` fails if either file drops below it or the two disagree. Lowering it means running both checks again: a PDF rendered inside the embedded browser, and `onnxruntime` imported after Qt. **A declared floor protects no one unless the build checks it** (`scripts/check_qt_runtime.py`, called by `build_exe.ps1` and `build_exe.sh` between `pip install .` and PyInstaller): `install.ps1` and `install.sh` never resolve a Python package — they download the release `build.yml` produced — so the Qt a user runs is chosen at build time, and editing the installers could not change it. The gate reads the floor from `pyproject.toml`, the file the build installs from, compares it with the installed distributions, and on Windows also reproduces the failure itself by importing QtWebEngine and then `onnxruntime` in a child process, the application's own order; a version number can only stand in for that. It was proven against a throwaway environment holding Qt 6.10.2, where both layers refused independently — the floor by number, the probe by the real `0xC0000005` — rather than only against the environment that passes. It emits `::error::` annotations under GitHub Actions. `OpalaTex.spec` is deliberately not the place for it: the build scripts invoke `pyinstaller ... main.py`, which regenerates that file on every build.
- **The four Qt packages share one ceiling, because nothing else keeps them on the same release** (`requirements.txt`, `pyproject.toml`: `<6.12` on `PyQt6`, `PyQt6-Qt6`, `PyQt6-WebEngine`, `PyQt6-WebEngine-Qt6`; `scripts/check_qt_runtime.py: mixed_release_problem`): PyQt6-WebEngine pins its own Qt binaries (`PyQt6-WebEngine-Qt6<6.12.0`) but asks only for `PyQt6>=6.2.0` and nothing of `PyQt6-Qt6`. PyQt6 6.12.0 and PyQt6-Qt6 6.12.0 were published before any PyQt6-WebEngine 6.12, so the next release build resolved Qt 6.12 next to WebEngine 6.11 and the Windows gate refused it: `DLL load failed while importing QtWebEngineWidgets: The specified procedure could not be found`. Checked statically against the `win_amd64` wheels: every Qt symbol WebEngine 6.11's DLLs import resolves against PyQt6-Qt6 6.11.2, while 6.12.0 no longer exports one of them, `QAccessibleInterface::QAccessibleInterface()` from `Qt6Gui.dll`. On Linux the same mix imported and rendered a page, so the snap would have shipped it unnoticed. The ceiling declares what was verified, as the floor does; it is raised only once the next minor of all four is published and passes the same checks. The gate also refuses installed packages that do not share one major.minor, and the snap build now runs the gate after its `pip install ".[gui]"` (the Windows-only import probe is skipped there). Covered by `tests/test_dependency_floors.py` and `tests/test_check_qt_runtime.py`.
- **A notice is classified from the whole dump, never from the copy shown** (`crash_report._session_section` / `_bounded` / `_is_native_fault`): the excerpt used to keep the *tail* of a run's section, and the fault classification read the excerpt. faulthandler writes the kind of fault and the faulting thread *first*, so the first dump that ran past the display limit (8 852 characters, a busy process with long paths) lost both lines, and a native fault was announced as a run that merely "ended without shutting down". Display now keeps the head, and classification reads the full section regardless of whether an excerpt was requested.
- **Tests**: `tests/test_crash_report.py` kills real child processes with real faults and asks, from the parent, the question the next launch asks; `tests/test_webengine_env.py` covers mode resolution, flag preservation and the Linux default; `tests/test_graphics_settings_api.py` covers the endpoints, including that reading the startup report does not consume it; `tests/test_app_restart.py` covers the environment handed to a restarted instance.

### 2.16.2 Startup Splash Screen

- **The window opens first; the agent runtime loads behind it** (`ide_server.start_gui_server`, `gui_src/public/splash.html`): measured on a warm Linux disk, the window used to appear only at ~5.4 s, and ~4.2 s of that was importing litellm (through agenticblocks) before anything was drawn. The window is now created at once on a splash page passed as `html=` (with `background_color` matching it, so pywebview's white default never flashes), and `_start_backend` — the agent runtime, the event hooks, the server thread — runs in pywebview's `func` thread, reporting phases through `window.opalatexSplash.status(...)` before `load_url`. Measured the same way: window with splash at ~1.1 s, editor ready at ~6.3–6.5 s, the same as before. The first phase is not reported because `evaluate_js` waits for the page's load and held the imports back by ~0.4 s.
- **What made that possible was an accidental import, not the window code**: `opalatex.config` imported `opalatex.tools` at module load only to call `get_project_path()`, which pulled agenticblocks and litellm into everything that reads configuration — `opalatex.cli` took 4.5 s to import. It now reads `tools` from `sys.modules`; until `tools` is imported no project can be set, so the answer (the working directory) is the same. `main.py` no longer imports litellm on its first line to set `suppress_debug_info`; `_start_backend` sets it, which now also covers the snap and pip GUI launches (it was PyInstaller-only) and no longer covers packaged `--cli` runs. `tests/test_startup_splash.py` keeps litellm off the path to the window.
- **One page, shown twice** (`vite.config.js: inlineSplash`): the same markup is inlined into `#root` of `index.html`, so the second half of the wait — parsing the bundle — shows the same screen until React's first render replaces it, instead of a blank page. The page's icon is a data URI because the native copy is shown before any server exists. A boot failure in the worker thread is shown on the splash (`opalatexSplash.fail`) and printed with its traceback, rather than leaving the splash spinning. A bundle built before the splash existed opens the window the old way and says so.
- **Not covered**: the splash has the brand's dark look in either theme, because the theme lives in the webview's `localStorage`, which Python cannot read before the window exists. The PyInstaller bootloader's own start time (unpacking a onedir bundle, loading Python) still precedes the window; PyInstaller's `--splash` would cover it, but only for those builds and not on macOS.

### 2.17 SyncTeX Forward and Inverse Search

- **A SyncTeX record names the line TeX was *shipping*, not the line the author wrote**: material collected into a box and typeset later is recorded under the line where the box was closed. A beamer frame is the extreme case — the whole body is emitted at `\end{frame}`, so a frame spanning ten source lines contributes exactly one recorded line — but the same holds for any paragraph, whose records only appear at the `\par` that ends it. `opalatex/synctex_parser.py: select_record_line()` therefore resolves a line with no record of its own **forward**, to the next recorded line, and only falls back to an earlier one when nothing follows. Picking the numerically nearest record instead sent the first half of every frame to the slide before it, and with `\pause` overlays in play it could miss by several pages.
- **Among the pages one record covers, the busiest wins, and a tie goes to the earliest**: a single recorded line can appear on several pages — the tail of a paragraph plus the heading that broke it, or one frame shipped once per overlay. Node count separates the first case (the page carrying the actual text wins); beamer ships every box on every overlay and only hides the unrevealed ones, so the second case ties, and the tie is broken towards the first overlay rather than left to node order.
- **This is what a recompile navigates with**: `EditorPanel` diffs the buffer against the last compiled content (`findFirstModifiedLine`), asks `/api/latex/synctex?action=tex2pdf` for that line once the new PDF is ready, and hands the result to `PdfPreview.scrollTo`. Partial compiles keep this honest because they only rewrite a wrapper around the *main* file — the edited file's own line numbers are never shifted.
- **The interface scale does not enter the mapping, and must not**: forward search stays inside the zoomed subtree end to end — `offsetTop`, `clientHeight`, `scrollTop` and the highlight's `top`/`height` are all app CSS pixels, so the `zoom` multiplies them together. The two places that do cross the boundary convert explicitly: inverse search turns the double-click's viewport offset into app pixels with `viewportPxToApp` before dividing by the PDF scale, and the current-page indicator compares page rects against the container's **rect** height, never `clientHeight` — mixing a viewport `top` with a CSS `clientHeight` put the pane's "center" at 1/scale of the way down and reported the page above the visible one at any scale above 100%. Editor font size never enters it at all: line numbers come from Monaco's model, and Monaco divides out its own `getBoundingClientRect()`/`offsetWidth` ratio, which under CSS `zoom` is exactly the interface scale.
- **A standalone PDF resolves only through its own SyncTeX file**: when the viewer shows a `.pdf` directly, `/api/latex/synctex?action=pdf2tex` uses the companion `<stem>.synctex.gz` (`synctex_parser.companion_synctex_path`) and nothing else — never the project's main document or the last compile's `last_synctex_path`, which would map a click on an unrelated PDF into another document. No companion file, or a recorded source that does not exist on disk (a SyncTeX written on another machine), answers 404 so the editor opens nothing. The embedded preview beside a `.tex` file keeps the main-file resolution above. Covered by `tests/test_synctex_standalone_pdf.py`.

- **Beamer `fragile` frames resolve through the frame, not the `.vrb`**: beamer writes the body of every fragile frame (the ones holding `lstlisting`/`verbatim`) to `\jobname.vrb` and `\input`s it, so SyncTeX records that material against the `.vrb`. Tectonic keeps it in memory and records it with an **empty** name, which the endpoint used to join onto the project path — Ctrl+click in a listing opened an empty editor tab; pdfTeX names it, but the file on disk holds only the last fragile frame. `find_source_line` treats an unnamed or `.vrb` input as generated: it finds the frame through the page's record on its `\end{frame}` line (where beamer ships the frame) and maps the `.vrb` line back with beamer's own layout (`beamerbaseframe.sty`/`beamerbaseverbatim.sty`: a synthetic `\frametitle` line for a lone title argument, lines copied one for one after it, comment lines consumed by `\@ifnextchar` look-ahead dropped). The layouts were checked line by line against Tectonic's `--keep-intermediates` output. Material from a generated file that is not such a frame raises `GeneratedSourceError` and answers 404, and the project-mode endpoint, like the standalone one, never returns a source file that does not exist. Covered by `tests/test_synctex_beamer_fragile.py`.

- **Tests**: `tests/test_synctex_forward_search.py` covers the forward resolution, the fallback past the last record, the overlay tie, and a per-line article where the exact match must still win.

### 2.18 Presentation Editor (`gui_src/src/slides/`)
The `.jpt` file itself is specified normatively in [docs/specs/jpt_format.md](docs/specs/jpt_format.md) (with a writer schema in [docs/specs/jpt.schema.json](docs/specs/jpt.schema.json)): the field reference, the invariants a change may not break, and the checklists for adding a field or a whole element type. This section records why the editor is built the way it is; that document records what the format is.

A WYSIWYG deck editor in the spirit of Google Slides, mounted by `EditorPanel.jsx` for any file ending in `.jpt` (JSON PresenTation). It is the one editing surface in the app that is **not** a view over LaTeX: slides are a *canvas* format — absolutely positioned boxes the user drags — and neither LaTeX nor Markdown, both flow formats, can express that without loss. Trying to make Beamer or Markdown the storage format would have made every drag a lossy operation, so the deck has a format of its own.

- **Created from the file explorer's context menu** ("New Presentation", next to "New file" and "New directory"), through the same inline creation row and the same `/api/file/write` endpoint as any other file — the same flow with a different seed, not a parallel one. All three create in place, as modern editors do: a provisional row (`components/InlineCreateNode.jsx`) opens inside the target directory of the workspace tree, expanding its ancestors, with the name field focused; Enter, Tab or Shift+Tab creates, Escape or a pointer press outside the row cancels, and a rejected name keeps the row open with the reason under it. Tab counts as confirming because leaving the field with the keyboard is the user's answer; before it did, Tab moved focus out while the row stayed open, so the following Enter activated whatever control had received focus and nothing was created. Losing focus alone deliberately does nothing: the IDE moves focus programmatically (the editor focuses a file it has just opened), and committing on blur created the pre-filled presentation name without the user ever confirming it. Path resolution and the suggested name live in `utils/inlineCreate.js`. Creation sends `exclusive: true` to `/api/file/write` and `/api/file/mkdir`, which then answer 409 for an existing path instead of overwriting it (plain calls keep their overwrite contract for saves). A presentation's row starts with a unique suggested name ending in `.jpt` (`presentation.jpt`, `presentation-2.jpt`, …) with the stem selected, and the `.jpt` suffix is still appended when the user removes it, since the extension is what routes the file to this editor and requiring the user to remember it would make creating a deck strictly worse than creating a file. The file is written with a real starting deck, so it opens on a title slide rather than on an empty document.
- **The extension is `.jpt`, one suffix rather than two.** The logical content is JSON, but the physical file is a ZIP package owned by the presentation editor — a plain `.json` stays a text file and a generic `.zip` stays an archive. The single suffix makes a presentation name, sort, filter and round-trip through a file dialog like `.tex` and `.md`. Textual surfaces receive the package's `deck.json`, so JSON syntax highlighting and readable checkpoint diffs are retained.
- **`deck.json` is the model; the package carries its local assets** (`opalatex/jpt/package.py`). `model.js` still defines `{version, title, width, height, theme, slides[]}` and parses that JSON directly. The ZIP adds `mimetype` plus stored `assets/<sha256>.<ext>` members referenced as `jpt:assets/…`; it does not add another slide representation. Images, backgrounds, video posters and local video files become internal members on every save. Content addressing deduplicates repeated assets, and keeping already-compressed media uncompressed in ZIP permits efficient seeking.
- **The server hides the container boundary from the editor.** `/api/file/read` extracts only `deck.json`, so opening a deck never loads a packaged film into JavaScript; `/api/file/write` packages and atomically replaces it, returning canonical JSON so the live buffer immediately uses `jpt:` references. `/api/jpt/asset` validates the member name and serves it with HTTP byte ranges, which lets a `<video>` seek without unpacking the document. `SlideEditor` needs both project path and deck file path to resolve an internal URI. Legacy plain-JSON JPT files remain readable and their first save upgrades them to the package.
- **Round-trip remains an invariant at both layers.** `parseDeck` → `serializeDeck` is byte-exact for `deck.json`, **unknown keys survive**, and known keys keep a fixed order. The package writer fixes timestamps and member order so a no-op rewrite is byte-deterministic, preserves safe unknown package namespaces, and writes to a verified sibling temporary before `os.replace`. A structurally broken but parseable logical deck is repaired rather than rejected by the editor; an unsafe package, missing local asset or dangling internal member fails without touching the original.
- **Every mutation is a pure operation** (`addSlide`, `updateElement`, `reorderElement`, ...) returning a new deck with structural sharing. This is deliberately the vocabulary an AI agent will be given later: one validated, diffable entry point per edit rather than free-form mutation of the JSON. Undo/redo is therefore a stack of deck references held by `SlideEditor.jsx`, not a text-buffer history — a user who moved a box expects Ctrl+Z to move the box back.
- **A live drag is local state, not a deck edit** (`SlideCanvas.jsx`). Committing every pointer move would re-serialize the whole document dozens of times a second and put one undo entry per pixel, so the canvas renders a `draft` rect and calls `onCommit` exactly once, on pointer up. The committed value is read from a ref rather than the state closure, which would race the last pointer move.
- **One renderer, three surfaces.** `SlideElementView.jsx` is the only place that turns model fields into pixels, and it is shared by the editing canvas, the thumbnail rail and presentation mode. A thumbnail that lays text out differently from the canvas is worse than no thumbnail, so none of the three is allowed its own renderer. Everything draws in deck units under a single CSS scale.
- **A list is a style on the box and a tab at the front of a line** (`model.js: textLinesOf`, `slides/lines.js`). Bullets used to be characters somebody typed — `"•  Point"`, and a sub-point was `"– …"` inside the same string — which is three defects wearing one coat: there is no style to change, no way to nest, and a marker the caret can sit inside. So the marker became a field (`bullet`: `disc`, `dash`, `number`, or none) and the nesting level became the number of tab characters in front of the line, capped at five levels. One string still holds the content, so there is no second representation to keep in sync, a tab is exactly what the user's Tab key means, and a diff of the file still reads like the slide. `textLinesOf` is the only place that turns that string into lines, markers and numbering — the canvas, the thumbnails, presentation mode, the HTML export and the PPTX export all ask it, and PowerPoint gets real `indentLevel`/`bullet` paragraphs rather than typed characters the recipient has to delete. Numbering counts per level and restarts a sub-list when its parent moves on; an empty line gets no marker and consumes no number, because an empty paragraph in a list is a gap the author left. Every line is drawn with a hanging indent, so a wrapped second line lands under the first word instead of under the bullet.
- **The text box the caret is in draws one element per line, and the browser is still what does the typing** (`slides/lines.js`). A marker in its own column and an indent per level cannot survive a flat `contenteditable`, so the editing surface renders the same line elements the finished box does. What it does *not* do is take over editing: Chrome splits a line on Enter and carries its level across, merges on Backspace, and handles selection, IME and drag-select better than a hand-written editor would, so the module checks the shape after every input and repairs it only when a paste or a select-all-and-type has flattened it — carrying the caret across as a `(line, offset)` pair read in the model's own terms. **Tab indents the line the caret is in** and Shift+Tab moves it back out; left to the browser, Tab moves focus out of the box, which committed the edit and selected the element the author was in the middle of typing in. That was the defect a user reported, and it is the one thing here no unit test could have seen, so the whole sequence is driven with real key and mouse events in `test/browser/run.py`.
- **The open box owns its own DOM, and React is kept out of it** (`lines.js: renderLines`). React reconciles against the last thing *it* rendered, which during an edit is the deck's committed text and not what the author has typed since — so any deck change while a box is open (a bold toggle, a list style, a line height) rewrote the box back to its committed content and threw the edit away. The editing surface is therefore rendered as an empty `contentEditable` and filled imperatively, which leaves nothing for React to reconcile; a small effect re-applies markers and indents when the box's *style* changes underneath the caret, and that path never touches the words. It is what lets every formatting control work while typing instead of closing the box to apply itself.
- **Undo inside a text box is the box's own, because the deck's cannot be and the browser's is not reliable** (`textHistory.js`). The deck's history is one entry per finished edit, which is right for a drag and useless for a caret: Ctrl+Z has meant "take back the last few words" since long before this editor. The browser's native `contenteditable` stack does approximately that, until the first script mutation — and this editor makes one after every keystroke, redrawing the marker column. Measured, with the editor's own handler switched off: typing, a line break and another word collapse into a single undo that also swallows the line, then the next one empties the box. So the box keeps a history over the only thing that changes while it is open — the string and the caret — as snapshots rather than diffs (a slide paragraph is bytes), coalescing runs of ordinary typing and breaking on a pause, a space, a line break, a deletion or a paste. **When it runs out, the keystroke is handed on**: the box commits and closes, and the deck's own undo takes the change before it, so Ctrl+Z never dead-ends. Ctrl+Y and Ctrl+Shift+Z are both redo, which is what the two halves of the world expect.
- **The classic shortcuts answer inside the box, or the browser answers for them.** Window-level shortcuts are switched off while the caret is in a text box, so Ctrl+B/I/U used to reach the browser, which wrapped the selection in a `<b>` — visible while typing and gone on commit, because the line reader keeps words and drops markup. They now toggle the box's own field, exactly as the panel buttons do: formatting is per box (format spec §13), and showing a run of bold that the format cannot carry is worse than not offering it.
- **Indent and outdent are commands, not only a key.** Tab is what a typist reaches for and Alt+Shift+arrows is what PowerPoint trained them on, but a command in the panel is what makes the feature discoverable — and it has two targets. With the caret in a box it moves the lines the selection touches, which the canvas answers through an imperative handle because that is where the caret lives; with the box merely selected it moves *every* line, which is the only sensible reading of "indent" with no caret and the one way to move a whole list in a single gesture. Every formatting control suppresses the default action of its own `mousedown`, so pressing a button does not blur the box, commit it and close it to apply itself — with two deliberate exceptions: the list-style buttons, which rewrite the text to take typed markers off it and so must act on committed text, and the `<select>`s, whose dropdown opens on exactly that default action.
- **The type controls the model already carried.** `fontFamily`, `lineHeight` and `valign` were fields the format defined, the exports honoured and the panel never offered — the same defect `slide.background` once had, and the reason §12.1 of the format spec makes a control part of adding a field. The panel now has a font stack list (the theme's font first, because that is what a box should use unless the author had a reason), the five line spacings every office suite offers rather than a spinner on a number nobody measures, and vertical alignment.
- **Markers already typed into a deck are stripped on the user's click, never on open** (`model.js: stripListMarkers`). Every deck written before the field existed carries its markers as characters, and switching a style on would draw a marker in front of a marker. Turning a list on therefore takes the old ones off — as an edit that appears immediately and undoes with Ctrl+Z. Doing it at parse time instead would rewrite a file nobody edited and break the round-trip invariant.
- **Direct manipulation lives in `geometry.js`**, free of React and the DOM so it can be tested: eight resize handles that anchor the opposite corner, aspect lock that follows the dominant axis, a minimum size clamped *after* the anchor (or a box shrunk from a north/west handle walks across the slide), and snapping to slide and sibling edges/centres with the guide the user snapped to reported back for drawing.
- **Rotation turns about the centre**, which is what makes it feel like turning the object rather than swinging it around a corner — and that choice is what the resize maths has to pay for. Dragging a handle on a rotated element needs two corrections, neither optional (`geometry.js: resizeRotatedRect`): the world pointer delta is rotated by `-rotation` so the handle drags along the element's *local* axes, and the result is then shifted by `u - R(u)`, where `u` is how far resizing moved the centre, to pin the anchored corner back to the world position the user grabbed. Without the second correction the opposite corner swings away as the box grows. The selection frame carries the same rotation, so the handles sit on the corners the user sees rather than on an invisible upright box, and the rotation handle rests above the top edge on a stem, reading 0° straight up so a grab never starts with a jump. Shift snaps to 15°.
- **Arrowheads are attributes of a line, not a shape kind** (`arrowStart` / `arrowEnd`), so one element covers a plain rule, a single arrow and a double-headed arrow without the user swapping shapes to change an end. `shape: 'arrow'` predates them and still means "a head at the end"; it is *read* through `model.js: arrowsOf` rather than rewritten at parse time, because normalizing it away would change the bytes of a file nobody edited and break the round-trip invariant. Every surface that draws a line — canvas, thumbnails, presentation mode, HTML and PPTX export — asks that one function, so none of them can disagree about where the heads go. PowerPoint's `beginArrowType`/`endArrowType` map onto the same pair.
- **A shape's border belongs to the shape**, not to a second element behind it: `stroke` and `strokeWidth` sit alongside `fill`, so moving or resizing a rectangle can never leave its outline behind. `model.js: borderOf` is the `arrowsOf` of borders — one function decides whether a border exists, and the canvas, the thumbnails, presentation mode and all three exports ask it, so none of them can disagree. A border needs *both* a colour and a width: a width with no colour is a border that was never configured, not a black one, and treating it as black is how `1px solid null` used to reach the DOM. On a line the same two fields already describe the line itself, so `borderOf` excludes lines and the properties panel offers thickness there and a border toggle everywhere else. The border is drawn **inside** the element box, which CSS gives the rectangle and the ellipse for free (`border-box`, and shrunken radii in SVG) but the triangle does not: an SVG stroke is centred on its path, so half of it would spill outside the `viewBox` and be clipped. `geometry.js: insetPolygon` moves each edge in by half the stroke width and intersects the offset lines, putting the outer edge back on the original outline. Its subtlety is the degenerate case — a border thicker than the shape can absorb does not flatten the polygon, it reflects it through the interior into a shape that is still convex, still inside the original and still wound the same way, so only a per-edge direction test catches it; that case collapses to the centroid, which reads as a solid patch of the border colour. PowerPoint is the one surface that cannot follow: it centres an outline on the shape's edge, and shrinking the box on export to compensate would move an element the user positioned.
- **The thumbnail rail is a listbox, because the arrows are bound twice.** Navigating the deck used to need the mouse: the thumbnails were clickable `div`s with no focus and no keys. The arrows could not simply be made global, because on the canvas they already nudge the selected element — so *focus* is what decides which of the two a key meant. The rail is a `role="listbox"` with a roving tabindex (one Tab stop, not one per slide, which in a forty-slide deck would put forty stops between the toolbar and the canvas), each thumbnail is an `option`, and its handler stops the event before the window listener that would also act on it. Up/down and left/right both mean previous/next — the rail is vertical, but the keys mean "previous slide" and "next slide", and a presenter reaches for whichever pair is under their hand. Home and End jump to the ends; Alt+arrows *move* the slide, which is the keyboard's counterpart of dragging a thumbnail and was otherwise the one thing only a pointer could do. Focus follows the selection so the next key continues from where the last one landed, the current thumbnail is scrolled into view whenever the slide changes from anywhere at all, and the duplicate/delete buttons appear on `:focus-within` as well as on hover — a hover-only control is one a keyboard user does not have. The pair that only a browser can settle — an element selected on the canvas, focus in the rail, arrows moving the slide and leaving the element where it was — is a check in `test/browser/run.py`.
- **Presentation mode leaves the interface scale behind.** The overlay and its fit-to-screen maths both cross the app's zoom boundary, and both have to be told: `.vscode-app` applies the accessibility scale as a CSS `zoom`, and viewport units — `inset: 0` on a fixed overlay included — resolve against the real viewport and are *then* multiplied by it, while `window.innerWidth`/`innerHeight` are reported unmultiplied and become CSS lengths inside the zoomed subtree. Getting either wrong makes the projected slide exactly `uiScale` times too large and pushes its bottom-right corner off the screen. The overlay is therefore sized with the app's corrected `--ui-vw`/`--ui-vh`, the fit divides through `viewportPxToApp`, and the slide counter is positioned inside the overlay rather than fixed to an unzoomed viewport. The result is that a projected slide is the same size at every interface scale, which is the point: the scale is an accessibility setting for the IDE's chrome, not for the deck being shown to a room.
- **The clipboard is the system's, not the editor's** (`model.js`, `slides/clipboard.js`). Copied elements are serialized into a tagged JSON envelope (`kind: 'opalatex.slides.elements'`) and written to the *system* clipboard, so a diagram copied in one presentation pastes into another window — an in-memory clipboard cannot cross two windows, which is the case users reach for first. The tag is what makes a paste unambiguous: `parseClipboard` returns `null` for text that is not an envelope rather than coercing it, and the editor then decides what that text becomes. Ids are regenerated at *paste* time, not at copy time, so the same payload can be pasted repeatedly without the second paste colliding with the first, and a payload written by another window can never carry an id that already exists in this deck. The whole group lands in one `addElements` call: one edit, one history entry, one re-serialization, so the undo that follows does not take a pasted group apart element by element.
- **A paste has to reach the clipboard twice, because neither mechanism works everywhere.** A native `paste` event carries `clipboardData` synchronously and is the only path that sees an image copied from another application without a clipboard permission prompt; the embedded QtWebEngine shell (§`_read_clipboard`) implements none of the async Clipboard API and dispatches no such event, and is reachable only through the backend endpoints `main.jsx` already patches `navigator.clipboard` onto. Ctrl+V therefore arms both — the native event, and a short timer that reads through the backend if that event never arrives — and a token plus a dedupe window makes sure exactly one of the two pastes. `utils/clipboard.js` gained `writeClipboard` for the same reason its `readClipboard` exists: copy and paste have to reach the same clipboard, so neither half is allowed to be the one that only works in a real browser.
- **Where a paste lands is a decision, not a default** (`model.js: pastePlacement`). A paste aimed at a point — the canvas context menu — centres the group on the cursor, because the user right-clicked where they want the content. A paste with no point behind it (Ctrl+V) puts the group back at its own coordinates, which is what makes copying an element from one slide to another preserve the layout; only when something already sits exactly there does it step aside, cascading by 24 units so a run of pastes leaves a readable stack instead of one invisible pile. Either way the group's *bounding box* is clamped rather than each element, so the arrangement the user copied survives the correction. Duplicate is the same path with no special case: the source occupies its own coordinates, so the cascade steps the copy aside on its own.
- **A pasted image is inlined and measured.** Images become data URIs in the live buffer, then package members on save; this avoids a temporary project file while still producing one self-contained JPT. They are sized to at most 60% of the slide width, with a floor: a favicon-sized paste left at its own pixel size would be an element too small to select, let alone resize.
- **The canvas context menu** (`SlideContextMenu.jsx`) offers Cut, Copy, Duplicate, Paste and Delete. A right click selects what it lands on *before* opening, so the clipboard items act on the element the user pointed at rather than on whatever was selected beforehand; on the background it clears the selection, which is what makes the menu offer only Paste there. Paste is never disabled — whether the system clipboard holds something usable cannot be known without reading it, and reading it is asynchronous, so greying the item out on a guess would hide a perfectly good paste. It reuses the IDE's `.vscode-context-menu` chrome and its positions cross the app's zoom boundary exactly as the file explorer's menu does; the chrome *on* the slide is the opposite case and carries explicit colours.
- **Equations are typed on the slide, not in a dialog** (`gui_src/src/slides/equation.js`, the `equation` element type). The demand this answers is the one Office set: insert a formula (toolbar, or **Alt+=**, PowerPoint's own shortcut), type, and watch the mathematics appear — never a source pane on one side and a preview on the other. So the *element on the slide is the preview*: `SlideCanvas` holds the formula being typed as local draft state and re-renders the element on every keystroke, while a small counter-scaled field carrying the LaTeX is anchored to the element itself (flipping above it and clamping to the slide, since the canvas clips). The draft reaches the deck only on Enter, Escape or blur, so a half-typed formula is never written to the file and typing a formula is one undo entry rather than forty. What is stored is always the **LaTeX source**, never a rendered picture of it: the source is what can be corrected a year later, what an agent can read, and what a newer KaTeX can re-render.
- **An equation box is fitted to its formula, and a resize handle scales the formula.** An equation has no independent width and height — its proportions belong to its content, like an image's — so after every change that can alter the rendered size (the source, the font size, display style, a drag) the editor measures the rendered MathML off-screen and applies the measurement *and* the change as a single edit. A resize gesture therefore locks the aspect while it is dragged and becomes a change of font size when it lands (`equationScaleFactor`, from the diagonal, so an edge handle degrades sensibly). Padding is a fraction of the font size rather than a fixed number of deck units: a constant padding is most of a small equation's box and invisible around a large one, and it made the box change proportions every time the user scaled it — measured, not assumed, by the browser check that compares the box aspect before and after a scale.
- **The equation's size is `fontSize`, exactly.** `katex.min.css` — loaded once for the whole IDE — sizes `.katex` at `1.21em`, so a formula in the editor would be a fifth larger than the same formula in an exported deck, which carries no stylesheet. `.deck-equation .katex { font-size: 1em }` neutralizes it on the slide and the exports emit the same rule, which is what lets the editor, the thumbnails, presentation mode, the `.html` deck and the printed PDF agree. The exports also ask for a font with an OpenType MATH table for the reason `mathFont.css` does, and the PDF path declares its own `@font-face` because it prints inside an iframe the app's font rules do not reach.
- **PPTX carries an equation as a PowerPoint equation** (`omml.js`). PowerPoint stores mathematics as OMML, which PptxGenJS cannot write, so the export used to ship the LaTeX source as a text box: honest, and useless — the reader got `\frac{a}{b}` on the slide and had to retype it. The conversion now goes LaTeX → MathML → OMML, and the middle step is the reason it is only a few hundred lines: KaTeX already parses the deck's LaTeX to MathML for every other surface in the app, so this module never parses LaTeX at all. LaTeX is a macro language with an endless surface; MathML is a fixed tree of about twenty element types, and only the second is something a bounded converter can get right.
  - **Never guess.** An element with no mapping — or LaTeX KaTeX cannot parse — is not approximated: the converter returns null, the LaTeX text box PptxGenJS already wrote stands, and the export note says how many formulas took that path. A formula that is *wrong* in PowerPoint is worse than one that is plainly still LaTeX, because only the second is visible as unfinished.
  - **Both, in one shape.** The equation shape is wrapped in `mc:AlternateContent`, which is how Office itself writes one: a reader that understands the `a14` extension gets an editable equation object, and one that does not gets the LaTeX. The fallback is the export's previous behaviour, kept rather than dropped.
  - **Written into the finished archive.** PptxGenJS has no seam for contributing an element, so the file it produces is reopened with JSZip and the marked shapes rewritten. Everything that generator computed — positions, fonts, relationships — is exactly what ships.
  - **No DOM.** The XML is parsed by a small reader in the module rather than by `DOMParser`, so the converter runs identically in the browser and under `node --test`, and the conversion of every construction the format supports is a unit test rather than something you find out about in PowerPoint.
- **A video is one `src`, and what it is gets derived, never stored** (`video.js`, mirrored by `video_source_of` in `model.py`). A slide's video is a link the user copied from YouTube or a file in the project, and those become completely different things — an `<iframe>` pointing at a player somebody else owns, or a `<video>` the browser decodes. Five surfaces have to agree about which: the canvas, the thumbnail rail, presentation mode, and the HTML and PPTX exports. So one function decides, exactly as `arrowsOf` and `borderOf` do. A second stored field could contradict the URL beside it; a derived one cannot. The deck also never stores a provider's *embed* URL, only what was pasted — providers change their embed paths, and a deck written today should still play in three years, which it will if what it kept is the address of the video rather than the address of a player.
- **Nothing plays on the editing canvas.** A live `<iframe>` swallows every pointer event that reaches it, so a YouTube embed on the canvas would be an element the author could no longer select, drag or resize. The canvas and the thumbnails draw a still — the poster, or a dark plate naming the video — and only presentation mode and the `.html` export pass `live`. Google Slides and Keynote do the same thing for the same reason.
- **A video autoplays muted or it does not autoplay.** Every browser blocks a video that starts with sound, so an unmuted autoplay is not a louder slide, it is a slide whose video never starts. The model keeps `muted` meaning what the author chose; the renderers mute an autoplaying video regardless, and the linter says so rather than leaving the author to discover it while presenting. YouTube's own quirk gets the same treatment: it loops a *playlist*, not a video, so a looping video is named as its own playlist.
- **Each export has a real answer for a video, and none of them is "it disappears".** The `.html` deck gets the actual player. The PDF gets the poster — paper cannot play — wrapped in a link to the video, because a printed deck routinely outlives the room it was shown in. PowerPoint gets a real media object: an *online video* for a provider link, the same object its own "Insert > Video > Online Video" produces, and an embedded media part for a file. The one gap is the cover shown before playback, which PptxGenJS can only write as a PNG, so a poster in another format is left out and PowerPoint draws its own play plate; that is recorded in the format spec rather than papered over.
- **A local film is packaged without entering the JSON.** On save, a project path or `data:` video becomes a stored, content-addressed ZIP member and `deck.json` keeps only its short `jpt:` URI. The editor resolves that URI through `/api/jpt/asset`, including HTTP `Range`, so opening and seeking do not parse or inflate the whole film. A YouTube/Vimeo URL remains a link to that external service; no package can make a hosted player offline.
