- **Export** (`export.js`): `.pptx` through PptxGenJS (MIT), entirely in the browser — no server round-trip and, unlike Marp or Slidev, **no headless Chromium**, which is what keeps it working inside the PyInstaller/snap package; a self-contained `.html` deck with keyboard navigation and no runtime dependency; and PDF through the browser's own print-to-PDF, driven from a hidden iframe whose `@page` matches the deck aspect (a popup is blocked in the packaged app). PptxGenJS is dynamically imported and lands in its own chunk, so a user who never exports never pays for it.
- **The PDF export prints the iframe, and the window has to be told that.** The hidden iframe is what carries the deck, and QtWebEngine routes `window.print()` from a subframe to `printRequestedByFrame`, not to `printRequested` — so for as long as `ide_server.py` connected only the second signal, "Export > PDF" did nothing at all in the packaged app, silently. Both are connected now, and the frame handler prints **the frame**, not the page: printing the page would produce a picture of the IDE around the slides. `QWebEngineFrame.printToPdf` honours the document's own `@page` size, which is what makes the pages come out at the deck's aspect instead of letterboxed onto A4 — verified end to end at 960×540pt for a 1280×720 deck. `QWebEngineFrame` and `printRequestedByFrame` arrived in Qt 6.8, so both PyQt packages have 6.8 as their declared minimum and an older runtime is rejected before it can silently print the whole IDE.
- **A PDF export finishes when the file finishes, not when `window.print()` returns.** The frame's versioned `name` carries a request id and the suggested file name, the only custom field Qt exposes with a subframe print request. The host keeps that request pending until `pdfPrintingFinished`, then dispatches a result event to the main page. The front-end therefore reports success only for a completed write, reports cancellation separately, and surfaces an asynchronous Qt failure; each hidden frame remains alive until its own result so a second export cannot truncate the first. A normal browser has no API that reveals the user's choice in its print dialog, so it says only that the dialog closed rather than claiming a file was saved.
- **The print document waits for its fonts and owns the same font definitions as the editor.** `load` fires before a web font arrives, and the math font is declared `font-display: block` — printing on `load` rendered formulas as blank space while the font was still in flight. The iframe requests the editor's web-font stylesheet, declares the bundled STIX Two Math face itself, and waits on `document.fonts.ready`. Ordinary text stacks also put STIX (plus installed math faces) before their final generic family: prose still uses the selected face, while unsupported Unicode operators and combining marks such as the `−` and arrow in `n⃗` fall through to a font that actually contains them instead of becoming missing-glyph rectangles. The canvas, its live text editor, presentation mode, standalone HTML, and PDF export all use the same resolver.
- **PowerPoint has no `object-fit`, so the export does the letterboxing** (`imageSize.js`). A picture handed to PowerPoint fills its frame, full stop, so a `contain` image exported as its own box comes out stretched to whatever shape the author drew — which it did, for every picture in every deck. The export now measures the picture and computes the rectangle itself: `contain` shrinks and centres, `cover` keeps the box and crops. Measuring is the browser's job where there is one and a header sniffer where there is not, so the behaviour is the same under `node --test`; a picture that cannot be measured is stretched, exactly as before, rather than failing the export.
- **Line spacing is exported in points, not per cent.** PowerPoint's percentage line spacing is a percentage of *single* spacing, which already includes the font's leading — about 1.2em — so a deck asking for `line-height: 1.5` and exported as 150% arrived at roughly 1.8em and overflowed the box it was fitted to. Exact point spacing is the same quantity CSS means, so the two agree. The text boxes also get their insets zeroed: PowerPoint pads a text box by 0.1in on each side and the editor pads by nothing, which was a fifth of an inch of width taken out of every box and enough to rewrap a line.
- **A third-party generator's escaping bug is repaired in our own pass, not in the dependency.** PptxGenJS interpolates an external relationship target into the XML unescaped, so a YouTube embed URL — which carries its playback options as query parameters, and therefore `&` — produces a malformed `.rels` and PowerPoint refuses to open the deck at all. The repair is idempotent (an ampersand that already begins a character reference is left alone) and runs in the archive pass that is already open for equations, so it is correct whether or not the library is fixed upstream.
- **Picked images may begin as data URIs, but saved images are package members.** A project-relative `src` is also honoured while editing. The backend turns both into content-addressed `jpt:` assets on every save and returns the rewritten JSON; the status strip reports unsaved local dependencies as assets that will be packaged, rather than offering a browser-side base64 conversion. Standalone HTML/PDF export still uses `inlineDeckAssets` because an exported file cannot call the JPT asset endpoint.
- **Self-containment is unconditional for local assets.** There is no size limit and no `embed_images: false` escape: an image or video too large for base64 is the reason the ZIP layer exists. Every asset-bearing field is walked, repeated bytes are stored once, and a missing/escaping path or session-only `blob:` aborts the save instead of producing a package that promises more than it contains. `embed_images` remains only as an idempotent compatibility operation for older agent calls.
- **An export inlines package assets into the exported artifact** (`export.js: inlineDeckAssets`). Canonical decks refer to `jpt:` members resolved by the same callback as transient project paths. Standalone HTML cannot call back into the IDE, and the PDF print document must not race an image still loading, so export fetches each source once and converts it to a data URI without mutating the deck. A source that cannot be fetched costs that asset rather than the whole export and is reported; silently producing missing pictures would leave discovery to the audience.
- **Pointer coordinates cross the app's zoom boundary.** The IDE renders inside a CSS `zoom` (§2.16), so `clientX`/`clientY` and `getBoundingClientRect()` come back in real viewport pixels. Both the fit-to-stage scale and every drag delta convert through `viewportPxToApp` before becoming a CSS length or a deck unit; without it the slide is drawn `uiScale` times too large and paints over the panels beside it. Relatedly, the stage **clips**: a scaled element keeps its untransformed layout box, so a 1280px canvas at 40% still occupies 1280px of layout.
- **Gestures use window listeners, not pointer capture.** Capture keeps a fast drag attached to its element, but it also retargets the click that follows to the capture element. Window-level `pointermove`/`pointerup` survive a fast drag equally well and leave the click sequence intact.
- **Double click is computed, not read off the event** (`geometry.js: isDoubleClick`). `pointerdown` carries `detail = 0` in Chrome — the click count exists only on `mousedown`, `click` and `dblclick`, none of which the gesture layer listens to — so two presses are paired by element, elapsed time and distance instead. Mixing a `dblclick` handler into a pointer-driven gesture layer is the alternative, and it makes the two disagree about which element was hit. The press that opens the editor calls `preventDefault()`: left alone, the browser's default action moves focus to the nearest focusable ancestor *after* the handler has mounted and focused the `contentEditable`, and the resulting blur committed and closed the box in the same tick it opened.
- **The interaction layer is verified with real browser input** (`gui_src/test/browser/`, run with `npm run test:browser` at two interface scales, about 35 seconds). The pure suite covers everything that can be made pure; it cannot see whether a click reaches an element, whether a panel is covered, whether focus survives the press that opened an editor, whether terminal selection follows the pointer, or whether a projected slide fits the screen. Every defect this editor has shipped lived in exactly that gap, so the browser suite drives a headless Chrome over the DevTools Protocol with `Input.dispatchMouseEvent`, which delivers events the way the operating system does. That distinction is not academic: an earlier throwaway probe used synthetic `dispatchEvent` and passed against a broken double click, because it set a field (`detail`) that real Chrome always reports as `0` on `pointerdown`. A test that invents its own inputs proves nothing. The one step that does build its own event is the image paste, and only because no DevTools command can put a *file* on a headless browser's clipboard: what is under test there is what the editor does with the payload, not the input that delivered it, and a `DataTransfer` holding a `File` is exactly the object Chrome hands over. The harness supplies its own `navigator.clipboard` for the same reason `main.jsx` does in the packaged app — headless Chrome refuses `readText()` outright, and the real app does not use Chrome's clipboard either, so reproducing that substitution is what makes the clipboard checks both runnable and faithful. Each shipped interaction regression is replayed against the suite and fails a distinct check, so the coverage is demonstrated rather than assumed. The harness lives outside `src/` and `vite build` takes only the root `index.html`, so none of it reaches a release.
- **Editing chrome is scale-compensated and theme-independent.** Handles, outlines and guides multiply their lengths by a `--deck-chrome` custom property (1/scale) so they keep a constant on-screen size at any zoom, and they carry explicit colours instead of `--vscode-*` tokens — they sit on the slide, which is white regardless of the IDE theme, and tokens turned them into black blocks in the dark theme.
- **Conformance suite** (`gui_src/src/slides/__tests__`, run with `npm run test:slides` — `node --test`, no added dependencies): round-trip byte-exactness including unknown-key preservation, repair of malformed decks, the invariants of every model operation, the resize/snap geometry, and the clipboard payload and paste placement.
- **A slide background is a colour with an optional picture over it** (`model.js: backgroundOf`, `SlideElementView.jsx: SlideBackground`). The colour existed in the format from the start and had **no control anywhere in the editor** — a capability the file could carry and an agent could set, that the user could not reach; the properties panel now shows it whenever nothing is selected, which is when the slide is what the user is working on. The picture is three separate fields (`backgroundImage`, `backgroundFit`, `backgroundOpacity`) rather than a polymorphic `background`, so a deck written by this build still opens in an older one: unknown keys survive (I2) and the older renderer draws the colour, where a `background` that had become an object would reach its CSS as `[object Object]`. Opacity is what makes the feature usable rather than decorative — a photograph at 40% over a solid colour is a slide you can still read text on, and the linter says so when it is not (`text-over-picture`). The picture is an `<img>` layer under the elements with **`pointer-events: none`**: the canvas decides a press is on the background by testing `event.target`, so a layer that took the press would silently break click-to-deselect — which is what the browser suite checks, because nothing pure can see it.
- **Theme chrome is theme data, not elements** (`model.js: chromeOf`, `SlideElementView.jsx: SlideChrome`). Beamer's headline and footline — the coloured band carrying the frame title, and the strip with the deck title and slide number — are drawn from six theme fields rather than injected as elements on every slide. Elements would be five things to keep aligned per slide, one thing to drag away by accident, and a set to find and delete when the theme changed. The header is drawn **only on slides that have a title**, because the band is the title's background: that is what keeps a cover or a section divider from carrying an empty coloured bar, and it is what Beamer does.
- **A text element has a `role`, and that is how a theme colours titles without colouring everything.** `theme.titleColor` reaches a box only when its `role` is `title`; a title's own explicit colour still wins over both. Applying a theme therefore *marks* each slide's title — the single bold text box inside the band the grid reserves — because a deck written before roles existed has none, and without that pass a white title colour would leave every title in dark ink on its own header band. The layout engine tags titles as it builds them, so an agent-authored deck never needs the heuristic.
- **A slide's background has three states, not two.** `""` inherits the theme's picture, `null` is an explicit *no picture* that overrides it, and a string is the slide's own. Without the third state a deck with a themed photograph could never have one plain slide for a dense table.
- **Known gaps**: PowerPoint takes a background colour or a picture but not a picture over a colour at an opacity, so a dimmed background exports to `.pptx` at full strength; `.pptx` export writes an equation as its LaTeX source rather than as a PowerPoint equation (OMML), since PptxGenJS cannot produce one; a standalone `.html` deck opened on a machine with no OpenType MATH font installed draws stretchy delimiters (the brace of `cases`, tall parentheses) at single-line height — the editor and the PDF path load STIX Two Math, but embedding it would add some 740 kB of base64 to every exported file, so the export asks for it by name and falls back; single selection only (`geometry.js` already has the marquee hit-test for multi-select, and the clipboard format is already a *list* of elements, so multi-select is the only thing standing between it and copying a group); slides themselves are duplicated rather than copied and pasted, since the thumbnail rail has no selection of its own to hang a clipboard off; no slide layouts/masters or theme editor; no charts or tables; text formatting is per-box rather than per-run, so one text element cannot mix bold and regular; `.pptx` **import** is not implemented, only export. Move-time snapping and `clampToSlide` use a rotated element's *unrotated* box, so a turned element snaps to where it would be upright — visible only at large angles, and preferred over snapping to a bounding box that grows and shrinks as the element turns.


### 2.19 Presentation Authoring API for Agents (`opalatex/jpt/`)

The counterpart of §2.18 on the backend: what an agent uses to *write* a deck. Its shape follows from one observation — a model handed the raw format writes plausible JSON and an unusable presentation, because the failure mode of machine-authored slides is not invalid JSON, it is layout. Text past the edge of its box, a title 40 units lower on one slide than the next, 14-unit type nobody at the back of the room can read: none of that is a format error, and none of it is visible to a JSON schema.

- **The author states intent and never picks a coordinate** (`layout.py`). Ten layouts — `title`, `section`, `bullets`, `text`, `equation`, `image`, `two_columns`, `image_text`, `quote`, `blank` — are compositions of *regions* (from one grid, shared by every slide) and *blocks* (bullets, a paragraph, a formula, a picture). Font sizes are fitted to the text rather than the text trusted to fit the font, so a long title shrinks and a long paragraph reports that it cannot fit instead of being clipped. Because every layout draws its title in the same region, a deck looks deliberate for the same reason a template does.
- **"Perfect" is checkable, or it is a wish** (`lint.py`). The linter is what makes the claim mean anything: text that overflows its box, elements off the slide or on top of one another, type below the readable floor, contrast under WCAG's 3:1 for large text, a missing image file, `$…$` delimiters wrapped around a formula that is already in a math field, a title that drifts between slides. `create_presentation` refuses to write a deck with **errors** and writes one with warnings while reporting them, so the agent finds out from the tool rather than the user finding out from a projector. The generator is held to its own checker: a test asserts that every layout produces a deck the linter passes clean.
- **Two implementations of one format, with the authority split written down** (`model.py`). The editor's `model.js` is the reference implementation of the *reader*; this is the reference implementation of the *writer*, and `docs/specs/jpt_format.md` is the contract both obey. `serialize()` here is byte-identical to `serializeDeck()` there — including JavaScript's habit of printing `1` where Python prints `1.0`, which would otherwise make the editor's first save a spurious diff — and a test proves it by round-tripping a Python-built deck through the real JS serializer whenever Node is present.
- **The author is strict where the editor is lenient.** The editor repairs a malformed deck because a user must never be locked out of their own file (§2.18); an agent gets an exception, because a silently repaired deck is a defect it never learns about. The line is *absent is a default, present but wrong is wrong*: a file that omits `rotation` reads fine, a file that says `"rotation": "left"` does not.
- **Text is estimated, and everything downstream knows it** (`metrics.py`). Measuring text properly needs the browser the editor runs in, so this side uses advance-width tables and a safety margin. The estimate is only ever used to make type *smaller* or to raise a warning, never to trim content — and for equations, where no table would help, the box is provisional by design because the editor re-fits it on open (format spec §4.2).
- **The escape hatch is inside the guarantees, not outside them.** Any slide may carry raw `elements`, because a layout vocabulary that cannot be escaped becomes a cage the first time someone needs a diagram. What comes through it is validated and linted like everything else, so the hatch moves responsibility rather than removing it.
- **A deck an agent writes uses the same package contract as the editor** (`opalatex/jpt/package.py`). `create_presentation`, `edit_presentation`, theme changes and generic full-file writes all finish through `write_packaged_jpt`: every local image, background, poster and video is copied into the document, repeated bytes are deduplicated, and the source file remains where it was for later editing. There is no 4 MB exemption or follow-the-old-file convention; self-containment is a property of the format. Missing/outside paths and dangling `jpt:` members fail the atomic write. The legacy `assets.py` data-URI helpers remain useful to exports and compatibility callers, but no longer define on-disk storage.
- **The author writes items, not markers** (`layout.py: bullet_text`). `bullets_element` used to prefix every line with `"•  "`, which is where most of the decks carrying typed markers came from; it now sets the `bullet` field and writes the words. A sub-point is `{"text": …, "level": 1}` or a string that carries its own leading tabs — two spellings of one thing, because an author writing an outline should not have to learn a representation — and `bulletStyle` picks `disc`, `dash` or `number`. The estimator was taught the same geometry (`metrics.wrap_lines(..., insets=…)`): a marker column and an indent are width the words do not get, and a list fitted against the whole box is one that overflows on the projector with nothing in the JSON to point at. Because both sides must read a list identically — this one estimates, the editor draws — a test runs the editor's own `textLinesOf` under Node and compares it line for line with `model.text_lines`.
- **`set_presentation_theme` is the agent's half of the store.** It takes a theme by name — an unknown name answers with the catalogue, so discovery needs no second tool — and/or explicit fields validated against the same `THEME_FIELDS` the sidecar reader uses. A theme's picture is packaged on save like every other local asset an agent puts in a deck.
- **Legibility over a picture is measured, not assumed** (`opalatex/jpt/imagestats.py`). The linter used to warn about *any* text over *any* background picture, on the grounds that contrast against a photograph is not a computable number. That was a confident answer to the wrong question: most slide backgrounds are pale artwork chosen precisely so text can sit on them, and warning about every one of them teaches the reader to skip the linter. PyMuPDF is a hard dependency and the picture is right there, so the question is now answered directly — the picture is decoded, the region under the *glyphs* (not the box: a title box spans the slide and its letters do not) is sampled through the same `fit` the renderer applies, and the contrast against the ink is computed per sample. The finding is raised when more than 6% of that area falls under 3:1, which is a number a reader would notice, where an average would hide a dark stripe in a pale picture. Only an undecodable source — a remote URL — still returns the honest "check this yourself".
- **Backgrounds are authored, not only edited.** The outline carries `background`, `backgroundImage`, `backgroundFit` and `backgroundOpacity` per slide and deck-wide through `theme`, and `set_background` changes them afterwards — with `"all": true` writing onto the theme, the same two choices the editor's own control offers. The linter checks what it can (`missing-background`) and is honest about what it cannot: contrast against a photograph is not a computable number, so the colour check is skipped for that slide and `text-over-picture` is raised instead of a confident wrong ratio.
- **`edit_presentation` is the §9 operation vocabulary, exposed.** Add or delete or move a slide, patch or reorder an element, set notes or a background — the same names the editor's own model uses, so a change made through the tool and a change made by hand are the same change. A failed operation writes nothing: the file on disk is untouched and the agent gets a diagnostic naming which operation failed.
- **Converting an existing `.tex` deck is a skill, not a tool** (`skills/tex-to-jpt/`). The mapping from Beamer frames to slides is judgement — which frames are sections, which overlay build should become consecutive slides, what a `$x^2$` inside a bullet should turn into — and judgement belongs in instructions the model reads, not in a function. What is *mechanical* is a script beside it: `scripts/tikz_to_image.py` harvests the deck's own preamble (`\usetikzlibrary`, `\definecolor`, `\newcommand`) into a `standalone` document, compiles it with the tectonic OpalaTex ships, and rasters it through PyMuPDF — because a picture compiled without its preamble either fails cryptically or silently draws something else. The skill also documents what a `.jpt` cannot carry (overlays, `\ref`, themes) so the conversion says so instead of losing it quietly, and what the equation element's KaTeX actually accepts, verified against the shipped version.
- **Tests**: `tests/test_jpt_format.py` (the JSON model, schema and cross-language byte exactness), `tests/test_jpt_package.py` (container determinism, deduplication, safety and migration), `tests/test_jpt_api.py` (logical reads/writes and media ranges), `tests/test_jpt_authoring.py` (layout properties, every linter rule and the tools), and `tests/test_tex_to_jpt_skill.py` (the conversion skill).


### 2.20 Command-Line Interface (`opalatex/cli.py`, `opalatex/cli_render.py`, `opalatex/cli_commands.py`)

The CLI is a **front-end**, not a second product. `opalatex --cli` starts an interactive
REPL, `opalatex -p "<prompt>"` runs one turn and exits, and both drive the same
`agent_stdin.handle_run` the desktop window drives. The agent workflow is unchanged: the
fixed MemGPT chat-orchestrator converses and delegates to skills through `run_skill`.

- **One turn engine.** The REPL previously called `memgpt.run()` directly, which was a
  thinner, parallel implementation of a turn: no streaming, no tool trace, none of the
  empty-response or serialized-tool-call recovery, no interrupted-turn persistence, no
  context measurement, no mode restoration. All of that lives in `handle_run`. Routing
  the REPL through it is what keeps the two front-ends from drifting, and it is why a
  fix to turn handling reaches both.
- **One event stream, two renderers.** `agent_stdin.print_event` publishes every
  streamed chunk, thought, tool call, problem and final response. `ide_server` installs
  a hook that forwards them to the browser; `cli_render.install()` installs one that
  renders them to a terminal. With no hook, `print_event` falls back to writing the raw
  JSON protocol to stdout — which is what the CLI used to display, one protocol line per
  token.
- **Rendering contract**, carried over from the GUI: streamed text is printed raw and
  never parsed as Markdown while partial; the final `agent_response` is not printed
  again when the stream already showed it, because there is exactly one user-facing text
  channel. `stream_retract` (an orphan `</think>` proving published text was reasoning)
  erases the text when it is still the visible tail of a TTY, and otherwise labels it —
  it arrives again as a `thought` either way. A `reflection` is held back one event: the
  closing iteration's reflection *is* the answer, and the desktop app can give it a
  panel of its own while a terminal has one column. Tool-call narration emitted for
  panel-based front-ends is tagged `auxiliary` and suppressed where the tool call itself
  is already shown.
- **Asking a human.** The two things a tool must ask — permission for an unsafe tool in
  `edit` mode, and approval of a plan — used to write straight to `_gui_input_pending`,
  a future only `/api/opalatex/input_response` resolved. Under `--cli` and `--stdin`
  nothing could answer and both waited out the 24-hour timeout.
  `agent_stdin.request_user_input` is now the single channel: it always publishes an
  `input_request` event, and the answer arrives from the GUI over HTTP, from the stdin
  protocol through an `input_response` command, or from a transport installed with
  `set_input_transport` (the terminal prompt). An unanswered request still times out;
  the caller decides what that means, because a tool defaults to refusing and a plan
  defaults to not approved. The turn's own input hooks (`agent_stdin._turn_ask_hook`,
  `_turn_confirm_hook`, `_turn_interactive_terminal_hook`, behind `ask_question` and
  `run_interactive_command`) go through the same channel; they had kept registering
  their futures by hand, so `ask_question` still hung in the CLI. Free-form requests
  (`ask`, `interactive_terminal`) carry no yes/no choices, matching the desktop
  contract (`normalizeInputRequest`). The terminal transport answers each type with
  the value the desktop dialog would send: the label or typed text for a question, a
  JSON array for a multi-select (`formatAskResponse`), and for an interactive command
  it runs the command in the terminal itself and returns `yes`/`cancel` after asking
  the user how it went.
- **Background commands.** `run_background_command` sends the command to the IDE's
  main terminal over HTTP. A front-end without that terminal installs a runner with
  `agent_stdin.set_background_command_runner`; the CLI's starts a process whose output
  goes to `<project>/.opalatex/background/command-<time>.log` and stops it when the
  session ends (`cli_render.uninstall`).
- **Interruption.** `Ctrl+C` cancels the turn's task — the same thing
  `/api/opalatex/interrupt` does — so `handle_run` records the interruption and keeps
  what the agent had written. It used to break the REPL loop, so interrupting a long
  turn quit the application. `/resume` continues from the stored turn, matching the
  markers in `agent_stdin.TURN_MARKERS` exactly as the window's Continue button does.
- **stdout ownership.** The JSON line protocol owns stdout, and `agent_stdin` used to
  claim it at *import* time by assigning `sys.stdout = sys.stderr`. The REPL imports that
  module to reuse the turn engine, so every Rich frame the CLI drew went to stderr and
  `opalatex --cli > log.txt` captured nothing. The redirection now belongs to
  `claim_stdout_for_protocol()`, called by `start_stdin_server()`.
- **Project selection mirrors the window.** Projects and the model catalog are global
  (`~/.opalatex/sessions.db`, `models_store`), so both front-ends already share them; the
  REPL just never showed them and read as a different tool. An explicit `--project` wins.
  Otherwise `opalatex --cli` opens on the project list (`cli.project_picker`): the
  registered projects in the window's order (most recently updated first), plus New
  project, Import an existing project (`project.import_project`, the desktop Import), and,
  when the working directory is not a project, Create a new project in this directory.
  The working directory only preselects the answer: its project when it holds one
  (imported on choice if unregistered), otherwise the first project whose folder exists,
  which is the window's own fallback when it remembers no project. A project whose folder
  is gone is listed but refused, as in the window, with the `opalatex --delete` that
  removes it; every failed choice returns to the list. `--here` skips the list and uses
  the working directory's project (or creates one there). The one-shot `opalatex -p`
  keeps resolving from the working directory, since a script has nobody to show a menu
  to; in a directory that is not a project it asks: create here, create elsewhere, or
  exit. Bare `/load` in the REPL shows the same list. `--mode` is applied only when
  passed: writing the flag's default back on every start silently reset a project the
  window had left in `auto`.
- **The chat previews the reasoning; the panel holds it.** A turn's reasoning can run to tens of thousands of tokens, and
  rendering it inside a chat bubble is what froze the window on a long turn. While a turn runs, the chat keeps only the last
  `chat_thought_preview_tokens` of it in view (default **1000**, in the editor settings beside the reasoning-context size), and
  both the live box and each past answer link to the **Agent Thinking** panel, which shows that turn's reasoning *whole*
  (`thought_tail` with no size, `/api/chat/thoughts?max_tokens=0`). Nothing is capped, summarized or thrown away -- the chat
  simply stops being the place where a hundred thousand tokens are painted. The panel follows whatever the chat is showing:
  a merged reasoning entry keeps its **end** rather than its beginning (`gui_src/src/utils/logMerge.js`), or it froze mid-turn
  once the entry passed its size — every new chunk was appended and cut away again, so the panel sat on the opening lines while
  the chat preview moved with the stream; a new turn hands the panel back to the live stream; and switching chats loads that
  chat's own recent reasoning into it, since the transcript no longer carries any. This replaced a per-model reasoning budget that
  stopped a turn past a token ceiling: measured against this user's own history, legitimate turns reached 82k reasoning tokens
  and four of them would have been cut mid-work, which is the kind of artificial limit the design does not want.
- **Stored reasoning is read when it is opened, not when the chat is** (`ProjectStore.thought_windows` / `thought_tail`,
  `/api/chat/thoughts`). Reasoning used to be persisted once per streamed token (rows written before segments, below, still are), so a long chat holds hundreds of thousands of rows: one
  measured chat carried 115,605, and `/api/chat/history` serialized all of them — **24.3 MB and ~1 s on every open**, before the
  browser parsed it and paired each chunk to a message — for text that starts collapsed behind "AI Thoughts". The transcript now
  excludes `thought` (`list_activity(exclude_events=…)`) and carries instead one row per assistant message: how many chunks it
  owns and the id range they span. Expanding the panel fetches that one window, tail-limited by the same reasoning-context size
  the live stream uses. Measured on the same chat: **0.91 MB and 0.52 s to open**, 93 KB and 0.13 s to expand its largest
  window (60,698 chunks). Nothing is dropped — the rows stay in `project_activity`, the pairing rule is the one the panel used
  while it did this itself, and the cap that protects memory still cannot reach reasoning, which is what the chat-API tests
  guard. The Output panel's reasoning rows are the one visible loss on reload: they belong to the same excluded payload, and the
  live turn still fills them.
- **Reasoning is stored in segments, not one row per token** (`agent_stdin._buffer_thought` / `flush_thought_segments`,
  `THOUGHT_SEGMENT_MAX_CHARS` = 2000, `THOUGHT_SEGMENT_MAX_SECONDS` = 2.0). One row per token grew one project to 872k
  `thought` rows averaging 2.9 characters — most of a 454 MB database and of a 309 MB cloud export, since every row also carries
  its chat id, timestamp, agent and payload. Consecutive reasoning of one agent in one chat is now collected in memory and
  stored as one row when it reaches either bound, before any other activity is stored or any other event is published, before
  the turn's answer is saved, and in the turn's `finally` (so an interrupted turn's reasoning is there for "Continue"). The live
  stream is unchanged: every chunk is still published as it arrives; only storage is grouped. A segment keeps the timestamp of
  its **last** token (`append_activity(timestamp=…)`), because `thought_windows` pairs rows with answers by timestamp and a
  segment flushed after an answer was saved must still belong to it. Its payload is the last chunk's, so `thought_tokens` is the
  turn's count at the segment's end. Status lines (`auxiliary: true`) stay rows of their own, and each agent (orchestrator,
  `worker:<skill>`) has its own segment — workers publish from their own threads, hence the lock. Whitespace-only tokens are now
  kept inside a segment (they used to be dropped as blank, gluing paragraphs together); a segment holding only whitespace is
  dropped. `thought_tail` meets a preview budget *inside* a row, keeping its most recent part, so the returned text stays
  contiguous whatever the row size. What a crash can lose is the segment not yet stored. Existing per-token rows are left as
  they are: rewriting them would change their merge keys, and another machine still holding the token rows would merge every
  one of them back.
- **Each front-end is told about its own affordances only** (`opalatex/prompt_surface.py`). The orchestrator's SKILL bodies and
  tool list are shared, so anything added for one front-end used to land in the other's prompt. Two things belong to one surface:
  the commands the shared registry marks `cli_only`, which the desktop chat refuses outright, and `get_editor_state`, which
  reports what the user has open in the IDE — under the terminal it can only answer "nothing staged" or hand back the snapshot a
  desktop session left on disk, as though it were what the user is looking at now. The body is filtered by the *subject* of each
  item (the first backticked name of a bullet or of a comma-separated entry), so a line that merely mentions a name in passing
  keeps its own, and fenced examples are left alone; the tool list is filtered by name at the single point where both the
  orchestrator's and the worker's lists are composed, which keeps each prompt's tool section and its actual toolset in agreement.
  The registry decides, not a marker in the prose: marking a command `cli_only` drops it from the desktop prompt with no edit to
  the SKILL files.
- **Replies written for a terminal.** The skill bodies are written for the desktop chat,
  which renders Markdown and typesets math; in a terminal `**`, `$\frac{a}{b}$` and
  `![..](..)` are printed literally. `cli_render.install()` sets
  `agent_stdin.set_reply_surface("terminal")`, and `chat_orchestrator_system_prompt`
  then appends a last block telling the model its text is not rendered: plain prose and
  `-` lists, no Markdown headings/emphasis/tables/images, formulas in plain text/Unicode
  instead of LaTeX math, verbatim source only inside fenced code blocks. It governs chat
  text only; the files the agent writes keep their format's syntax. The prompt is
  rebuilt every turn, so the same chat reopened in the window gets the window's prompt.
- **Creating a project.** The main model comes from `--model` when passed, otherwise
  from the model catalog (the only entry is taken, several are offered as a choice). An
  empty catalog leaves the project without a model and says so; `--model` has no built-in
  default, because `DEFAULT_MODEL` stored on the project failed to connect for users who
  never registered it. The folder is checked with `config.project_dir_error`, which also
  probes `<folder>/.opalatex` (the desktop create-project endpoint uses the same check):
  under snap the home directory itself accepts files but not that hidden top-level
  directory, so the CLI re-asks, suggesting `<folder>/<project name>`. `/project new`
  (CLI only) runs the same creation from inside the REPL and switches to the new
  project; bare `/project` shows the current one. Opening a project (startup or
  `/load`) runs the same folder check and reports a folder that cannot hold the
  project's state, since projects registered before the check existed would otherwise
  fail only as "Permission denied" on every turn.
- **Discoverable help.** `/help` groups the commands into sections and prints each one's
  argument shape, and `/help <command>` prints its worked examples (`details=` on the
  registration). The listing used to go through Rich's markup parser, so every usage
  string written with brackets — `[n]`, `[auto|plan|edit]`,
  `[list | add key=value...]` — was read as markup and dropped: precisely the commands
  with a shape worth looking up showed none of it. Help text is now printed literally
  (`markup=False`), and `handle_slash_command` passes literal text through untouched and
  fences it, instead of re-reading it with the heuristics that turn ordinary command
  output into chat Markdown.
- **Shared command registry.** `cli_commands._registry` backs both the REPL and the
  GUI's `/`-commands (`agent_stdin.handle_slash_command`). Commands marked `cli_only`
  (`/chat`, `/resume`, `/thoughts`, `/tools`, `/compile`) are refused by the GUI door
  rather than silently doing nothing there, and are hidden from its `/help`. The
  orchestrator a `REPLState` may need is built on first use, so the GUI no longer
  constructs one per slash command.

#### 2.20.1 Registering a Model from a Text Front-End (`opalatex/cli_catalog.py`)

A usable model is two records, and both were writable only through the desktop Settings
dialog: a **provider connection** (label, LiteLLM provider, API base, key — credentials
live here, so rotating a key updates every model under it) and a **catalog model** (a
name under a connection plus the capabilities and inference parameters that describe it).
`/set-main-model` only stores an id on the project; with no catalog entry behind that id,
`config.get_agent_llm_kwargs` resolves no `api_base`, no key and no capabilities, so the
run reaches the provider unauthenticated and every per-model setting reads as unset.
`/set-main-model` and `/set-worker-model` therefore warn when the id has no catalog entry
(the id is still stored, since environment credentials can serve it), and every catalog
lookup that misses names the closest registered id (`cli_catalog.report_unknown_model`).

`/providers [list|add|set|remove]` and `/models <subcommand>` manage them with `key=value` fields. They are in the shared registry and
are **not** `cli_only`: they change a global store rather than terminal state, so they
work in the REPL, in `opalatex -p`, over the stdin protocol and in the desktop chat's
command box. Bare `/models` still answers what the current project runs on.

- **Arguments are `key=value` pairs**, quoted where they contain spaces, with aliases for
  the way the fields are spoken (`connection`, `thinking`, `profile`, `policy`,
  `context`). An unrecognised key is **refused with the list of known fields** instead of
  being forwarded to the provider as an inference parameter: unknown keys are exactly how
  a typo such as `num_ctxx=5` would become a setting that silently does nothing.
  Deliberate pass-through parameters are written `extra.<name>=<value>` and land in
  `extra_model_params`.
- **Ids follow the desktop form's rule**, now shared through
  `models_store.suggest_model_id` / `suggest_connection_id` rather than duplicated:
  `provider/name`, suffixed with the connection id when a different entry already holds
  that base id — the shape `resolve_runtime_model_id` strips back to `provider/name` for
  LiteLLM.
- **A model row never stores its connection's credentials.** `provider`, `api_key`,
  `api_base` and `connection_label` are joined from the connection on read
  (`_row_to_model`); writing them back would leave a copy of the key in the model row,
  stale the moment it is rotated. The desktop form does not send them either.
- **Editing a connection preserves its key** unless `api_key=` is passed empty, so
  relabelling does not silently de-authenticate every model under it. Deleting a
  connection still in use is refused, naming the models that block it.

- **Step by step.** `/add-provider` asks for what the desktop Add Connection form asks
  (label, LiteLLM provider, API key read without echo, base URL -- preset to the local
  Ollama URL for `ollama`/`ollama_chat`), shows the result and saves on confirmation.
  `/add-model` refuses with "use /add-provider first" when no connection exists, then
  asks only the main fields (provider connection, name, `num_ctx`, supports thinking);
  every other field keeps the default the desktop form starts from, the saved entry is
  printed in full, and the closing hint names `/set-model-field`. Both read the keyboard,
  so they are `cli_only`, and a stdin with nobody behind it (EOF) is reported instead of
  guessed at.
- **One field at a time.** `/set-provider-field <id> <field> <value>`,
  `/set-model-field <id> <field> <value>`, `/remove-provider <id>` and
  `/remove-model <id>` (underscore aliases too) are the `key=value` commands spelled for
  one change and reuse their parsing and validation, so an unknown field is still
  refused. A connection's id never changes (models refer to it) and its label/provider
  cannot be emptied. `/set-model-field` edits the catalog entry, shared by every project
  using the model; `/set-model-param` stays the per-project override.

Two reach fixes came with them, both general rather than specific to the catalog: the
stdin protocol gained a single `slash_command` command rather than one protocol verb per
feature, and `opalatex -p` dispatches a prompt beginning with `/` through the registry
instead of sending it to the model. Registering a model from a shell script is the same
need a one-shot prompt serves, and handing `/models add …` to the model as though it were
a question would have been the wrong action entirely.

#### 2.20.1 Distribution

The base install is the command-line interface: `pip install opalatex` or
`uv tool install opalatex` yields a working agent — projects, skills, tools, and LaTeX
compilation through a `tectonic` on `PATH` — with no desktop stack. The desktop
application is the `gui` extra (`pip install "opalatex[gui]"`), which the packaged
builds and the snap install.

Every Qt and pywebview import in the package sits inside a function of `ide_server.py`,
so the split is structural rather than conditional: `opalatex.cli`, `cli_render`,
`agent_stdin` and `memgpt_runtime` import none of them, and a test asserts it. Requesting
`--gui` without the extra fails with a diagnostic naming it; it must not fall back to the
CLI, which would substitute a different product for the one that was asked for.

Feature parity is complete for everything that is the *agent* (skills, file and document
tools, web search, image generation, Git and shadow checkpoints, Tectonic compilation,
cloud mirroring) and absent for everything that is the *editor* (PDF preview, SyncTeX,
rich-text and WYSIWYG modes, PDF annotations, presentation mode, dictation and
read-aloud), which the terminal has no surface for.

### 2.21 Mobile Companion App (`mobile/`)

An Android app that continues a project's work away from the computer: it lists the projects the desktop mirrors to Google Drive, shows their files (text, Markdown, PDF, images) and their conversations, and chats about the project with an agent that can read, search and — with approval — edit its files. It is standalone: there is no server and no connection to a running desktop; the cloud mirror (§2.15) is the only channel. Decisions are recorded in [docs/specs/mobile_app.md](docs/specs/mobile_app.md), the file contract in [docs/specs/chats_export.md](docs/specs/chats_export.md).

- **Stack**: React 18 + Vite + Capacitor 8, in JavaScript like `gui_src/`, with the same Markdown, KaTeX and i18n libraries at the same versions; English is the default and `pt-BR` is shipped. The native layer is one app-local plugin (`android/app/src/main/java/com/opalacoder/opalatex/OpalaNativePlugin.java`): Google authorization, Keystore-encrypted secrets, and streaming HTTP.
- **Google access is the desktop's `drive.file` grant, from a second client of the same Cloud project**: Google identifies the application that `drive.file` grants by Cloud project number, so an Android OAuth client (package `com.opalacoder.opalatex` + signing SHA-1) registered in the project that owns the desktop's bundled client sees the `OpalaTex/` folder the desktop created, and nothing else in the account. The app uses Google Identity Services' `AuthorizationClient`; no client id is compiled in and no refresh token is stored on the device. A client in a different Cloud project would see an empty container, which the project list says.
- **The phone writes one file of its own**: turns go to the device's journal, saved locally first and uploaded after each turn and on every sync; uploads are serialized because Drive allows two files with one name. Rows the export already holds are compacted out. The conversation view is a union of the export, this device's journal and other devices' journals, keyed exactly as the desktop keys rows (verified against the Python implementation on a 3,713-row export).
- **Its own agent, the desktop's contracts**: agenticblocks cannot run on the device, so `mobile/src/agent/` has OpenAI-compatible and Anthropic streaming adapters and a bounded tool loop (16 steps, a breaker at three identical failing calls). Unknown tools and malformed arguments are reported to the model, never substituted. A chat runs on a model picked from the chip at its top — any model of any connection configured on the phone, addressed by connection id and model name — or, until one is picked, on the desktop project's model (`settings.model`, litellm-style) resolved by its provider prefix; an unresolvable model is a stated reason with a way to pick one, never a fallback to another provider. Connections list their models, typed or loaded from the provider's model listing. Context is borrowed, not shared: the chat's `recursive_summary`, core memory, and the newest visible turns under a budget; `agent_state` is never written.
- **Model traffic is native, Drive traffic is `fetch`**: a web view is bound by CORS and not every provider allows browser origins, so the plugin streams provider responses natively; Google's APIs accept cross-origin requests. Cleartext HTTP stays disabled, so a LAN Ollama over `http://` is refused with Android's own error.
- **Edits**: `edit_file` (one exact, unique passage) and `create_file` show the exact change for approval, then re-check the file's md5 in Drive immediately before writing and refuse when it changed or, for a create, when a file appeared. Drive has no conditional write, so that narrows the race to one round trip, and the desktop's sync engine turns a remaining collision into a conflict copy. Nothing under `.opalatex/` is writable.
- **Trying again without rewriting history**: a question left unanswered is answered again in place (only the answer is written). Retrying an answered turn or editing a question continues in a branch — a new chat with a copy of the conversation up to that point (`turn.js: branchChat`) — because a journal can add rows but never supersede one; the desktop branches the same way for an edit of an earlier message.
- **Tablets**: from 720 px wide (`ui/layout.js: TWO_PANE_QUERY`) a project shows its list beside the open chat or file; the open file is held by the project screen, so it survives a rotation between the two layouts.
- **Screens stack, sessions persist**: Settings, a chat and the file viewer open over the screen beneath, which stays mounted; a project's session lives for the life of the app and is restored from the device cache (`session.restore`, no network) before each sync. Replacing the project screen with Settings used to unmount it and rebuild its session from nothing, so returning showed an empty project until Drive had been walked again.
- **Offline**: the project list, file index, opened files (by md5) and conversation documents are cached in IndexedDB, and a per-project *Available offline* switch downloads every viewable file (text ≤ 2 MB, PDFs and images ≤ 50 MB) after each sync, only where the md5 changed, pruning copies of removed files and stopping at the first network-wide failure; Android backup is disabled because the Keystore key that encrypts the API keys does not travel with a backup.
- **Builds and tests**: `npm test` (node --test: formats, keys, agent, Drive client against an in-memory Drive, sessions and turns end to end), `npm run test:browser` (headless Chrome at phone size with real input; Drive and the provider answered by interception), `npm run android:debug`. `.github/workflows/android.yml` builds on changes under `mobile/` and publishes a signed APK and AAB for `mobile-v*` tags, refusing to run without the signing secrets — the release key's SHA-1 is the one registered on the OAuth client, so an unsigned release would install and then fail at sign-in.

### 2.22 Docker Sandboxing for Agents (`agenticblocks/blocks/patterns/sandbox.py`, `opalatex/docker_sandbox.py`, `opalatex/sandbox_config.py`)

An opt-in, experimental engine that runs the processes agents start (Orchestrator and Worker alike) inside a per-project Docker container instead of on the host. **It isolates processes, not the project**: the project is bind-mounted read-write, and the file tools keep editing it on the host, so anything a command writes there (including `.git/hooks`) reaches the host.

- **Framework primitives (`agenticblocks/blocks/patterns/sandbox.py`)**:
  - `DockerContainerManager.ensure_container` keeps a named container running with a given specification (image, network, `BindMount`s, user, environment, workdir). A digest of that specification is stored in the `agenticblocks.sandbox.spec` label, and a container whose label differs is removed and created again, so a changed setting always takes effect. Creation is serialized per name in the process and retries once on a name conflict from another process; an `inspect` failure other than "no such container" is reported instead of being taken as absence. `pull` is passed to `docker run --pull`.
  - Mounts use `--mount type=bind,source=<native path>` (CSV-quoted), never `-v` with a converted path: the Docker CLI resolves native paths on every platform, including `C:\...` with Docker Desktop.
  - `exec_in_container` marks every exec with `AGENTICBLOCKS_EXEC_ID=<uuid>`. On a timeout `stop_exec` kills, as root inside the container, every process whose environment carries that marker (`/proc/*/environ`, two passes): killing the `docker exec` client alone leaves the command running in the container. The output produced until then is returned, and `stop_error` reports a failure to stop (e.g. an image without `tr`/`grep`).
  - `CommandExecutorBlock` runs a command locally or in docker mode, off the event loop; in docker mode the mounted directory (`volume_mount`, or the input's `cwd`) is `/workspace`, `cwd` is mapped into it, and each mounted directory gets its own default container.
- **Global configuration (`opalatex/sandbox_config.py`)**: `<opalatex home>/sandbox.json` with `enabled`, `image` (default `python:3.12-slim`) and `network` (`bridge` or `none`). Saving merges over the saved values. `tests/conftest.py` points it at an empty home in every test, since it is read on every command.
- **Project container (`opalatex/docker_sandbox.py`)**:
  - Named `opalatex-sandbox-<sha256(project_path)[:12]>`, kept alive with `tail -f /dev/null` so installed packages persist across turns. The project is mounted at `/workspace`; skill folders outside the project (`skills.skill_search_dirs`) are mounted read-only at `/opalatex/skills/<sha256(dir)[:12]>`, so skill scripts run there too.
  - **User**: on a POSIX host the container runs as the host's `uid:gid` with `HOME=/tmp`, because files a root container writes into the bind mount would belong to root on the host. Rootless Docker/Podman (`docker info` SecurityOptions contain `rootless`) and Windows keep the image's user, which those already map to the user. Consequence: `pip` installs go to the user site and `apt-get` needs a custom image.
  - The container is created with `--pull never`; a missing image fails at once and points to the Settings *Pull Image* button instead of downloading within the creation timeout.
- **Command routing (`opalatex/tools.py`)**:
  - `_run_captured` (`run_command`, `run_python_script`) runs in the container; timeouts produce the same message as the local path, with the partial output, or say that the processes could not be stopped.
  - `run_python_script` maps the script with `container_path` (project → `/workspace/...`, skill folders → their mount) and runs `python3`; a script outside both is refused.
  - `run_interactive_command` and `run_background_command` go through a terminal (bash/PowerShell in the IDE, the CLI's `sh`/`cmd.exe`), whose quoting rules differ. `sandbox_terminal_command` writes the command to `<project>/.opalatex/sandbox/run-<id>.sh`, which deletes itself when it starts, and returns an unquoted `docker exec [-it] -w /workspace <name> sh /workspace/.opalatex/sandbox/run-<id>.sh`. `-it` only for a terminal with a TTY (not the CLI's background runner). Starting the container happens in a worker thread, and a container that cannot start fails the tool call; it never falls back to the host.
  - The user's integrated terminal and the IDE's own compile/export (Tectonic, Pandoc, which are not in the image) stay on the host.
- **Snap (`snapcraft.yaml`)**: declares the `docker` plug (its slot is implicit on classic systems) and bundles the static Docker CLI in `$SNAP/bin`. The plug only lifts confinement; the socket's own `root:docker` permissions still require the `docker` group. Snap detection for this purpose is `SNAP_NAME == "opalatex"`, since a terminal of another snapped app exports its own `SNAP`.
- **Diagnostics (`verify_docker`, `/api/settings/sandbox/*`, Settings modal)**: CLI presence, daemon reachability, permissions and image presence, with copyable commands. On *permission denied* under Snap, `snapctl is-connected docker` decides between the plug and the group. When `/etc/group` lists the user in `docker` but the process lacks the group (`usermod -aG` only affects new login sessions, and a restarted app inherits the old session's groups), it asks for a new login instead of repeating `usermod`.

---

### 2.23 Main Agent and Helper Agents (`opalatex/memgpt_runtime.py`, `opalatex/skills.py`, `opalatex/tools.py`)

The chat-orchestrator is the **main agent**: it does the user's task, makes every change to the project, and is the only agent that speaks to the user. Other agents are **helpers** that do auxiliary jobs for it. The design replaced an orchestrator/worker split in which workers changed files on the orchestrator's behalf.

- **Why**: a worker's tool error reached the worker's model only, with the instruction to correct it silently, and the orchestrator received nothing but the worker's final prose. Measured in a real run, a `search_code` call failed on `bad escape \l` inside a worker, and the orchestrator was told "Done" with no trace of it. Annotating the worker's report with its errors would have had the opposite problem: an error the worker already recovered from would make the orchestrator redo or redelegate finished work. Moving every change into the main agent removes the problem at its source — write, compile and command errors happen in the loop of the agent that answers for them — and leaves helpers with jobs whose errors only affect the quality of data the main agent evaluates anyway.
- **Two kinds of skill, by the `role` frontmatter** (`skills.HELPER_ROLE`, `skills.INSTRUCTIONS_ROLE`): a `role: helper` skill runs as an ephemeral helper through `run_skill`; any other skill (the default, `instructions`) is loaded by the main agent with `load_skill` (`memgpt_runtime.build_load_skill_tool`), which returns the body for the main agent's prompt profile, its parent's body first when it `extends` one, the skill directory and its scripts — then the main agent does the work with its own tools. Each tool refuses the other kind with the route that works, and an unknown role is refused by both and listed as unusable rather than guessed. The bundled helpers are `staged-reader`, `elementary-ops`, `log-table-condenser` and `web-search`, and the Asset Store packages of the first three carry the same role, since an installed copy shadows the bundled skill.
- **Helpers return data, never changes** (`tools.get_helper_tools`, `tools._refuse_helper_project_write`, `prompt_profiles.HELPER_CONTRACT`): a helper gets the read and search tools, `web_search` (the `web-search` skill told its worker to use a tool the worker toolset never had), scratch-area writes and the command tools that run a skill's scripts. It does not get the tools that produce deliverables (documents, presentations, images, a compile) or drive the user's terminal. `write_file`, `edit_file`, `write_content_pos` and `replace_content_range` refuse, inside a helper, any path outside `<project>/.opalatex/` (resolved, so `.opalatex/../x` is outside), with a diagnostic telling it to report the exact change instead. `run_command` is not confined — a shell cannot be path-checked — so helper commands remain under the mode gate and the turn's checkpoints, as before.
- **The main agent knows what each helper can see** (`memgpt_runtime._skill_catalog`, `config.model_supports_vision`): each helper entry shows its model (resolved by `_helper_model_for`, the same function `run_skill` uses) and `vision: yes/no`. Vision is LiteLLM's registry or the user's `force_vision`, read from the parameters of the model that runs (`_helper_vision_params`: the main parameters for a helper on the main model, the worker parameters otherwise), so a declaration about one model is never applied to another. Inside a helper, `analyze_image` asks the helper's own model, and a helper is given `analyze_image` only when that model sees images; the main agent sends image work only to a `vision: yes` helper, or analyzes the image itself.
- **The main agent sees what went wrong, without being told it failed** (`AgentOutput.tool_calls` in agenticblocks, `memgpt_runtime._helper_tool_error_notes`): `LLMAgentBlock` records every tool call — index, tool, whether it produced a result, and the error — and `run_skill` appends to the helper's report a bounded list of the failed calls, each with how many calls followed and how many of those were successful calls of the same tool. The list states facts, not a verdict: whether the helper recovered is the report's to say, and a helper is told to name any part of its job a tool error prevented. The loop breakers (`_is_failed_worker_result`) classify the report alone, which is what `_skill_run_history` stores, so a recovered error never turns a successful run into a "failed" one.
- **Small models belong in helpers**: helpers run on the project's worker model unless their SKILL.md names another (`model: default` runs a helper on the main model; `web-search` does). Pointing the worker model at a smaller model, together with the `delegate` policy (§2.8), moves the bulk reading and research off the main model's tokens.
- **`search_code` says how it read the query**: the query is literal unless `use_regex=true`. Models wrote `a|b|c` without it, got "No matches", and concluded — in a recorded review of a paper — that citations were absent. A literal query containing `|` that matches nothing now says the `|` was matched literally and how to search for alternatives; the search itself is never converted. An invalid pattern fails before any file is read, with the regex error and the LaTeX-specific cause (a backslash must be doubled), instead of as an error "searching" a file.

### 2.24 Monitor Agents (`opalatex/monitors/`)

A monitor is a stored, deterministic watcher: *what* to check (`timer`, `http`, `command`, `file`, or an `llm` question), *when* (interval, optional first delay, `max_checks`, `ttl`), and *what to do* when its condition holds (`notify` — an OS notification; `resume_chat` — wake the chat's orchestrator; or both). It turns the orchestrator from strictly request–response into one that can wait without holding a turn open, and lets the user ask in the chat for something to be watched ("tell me whenever the dollar moves 10 cents"). Decisions and their reasons: [docs/specs/monitors.md](docs/specs/monitors.md).

- **Separate process, shared file.** Closing the window ends the server process (§2.1), so monitors run in a daemon of their own, `opalatex monitors daemon` (`daemon.py`), one per user (an OS-released file lock, `service.DaemonLock`). Every process — window, CLI, daemon and the turns it starts — coordinates through `<opalatex home>/monitors.db` (`store.py`): monitors and their history, wake requests, pending questions, the notification outbox, front-end liveness (`hosts`), per-chat turn leases and notification clicks. Registering or resuming a monitor anywhere calls `service.ensure_daemon_running`; the window also does it at start-up when monitors are active. The daemon exits after 15 idle minutes (nothing active or pending, and no notification in the last hour whose click it should still receive). **It restarts itself when the installed code changes** (`service.code_fingerprint`: version, newest source mtime and count, a frozen executable's mtime, the snap's `current` revision; checked every 30 s before any work): a long-lived daemon otherwise kept the old code in memory across an update, and a monitor registered with a newer option (`chat: new`) was carried out by code that ignored it. On POSIX it `exec`s `service.daemon_argv()` (the same PID, so systemd and launchd see one service); on Windows it starts a detached successor and exits.
- **Start at login** (`service.autostart_backend`, on by default, toggled in the panel and `opalatex monitors service`): a `systemd --user` unit on Linux (XDG autostart where there is no user systemd), the snap's own `autostart:` app fed by a desktop entry in `$SNAP_USER_DATA` (a confined snap can neither write `~/.config/systemd` nor run `systemctl`), a LaunchAgent on macOS, and the `HKCU\…\Run` key on Windows (a logon-triggered scheduled task needs elevation on many systems). The unit carries `OPALATEX_HOME` when it is set, so the daemon reads the same data folder as the app. The uninstallers remove it. **A login service belongs to one data folder**: there is one per user but data folders can be several, so each service records the folder it serves (a comment in the unit, `X-OpalaTex-Data-Folder` in the desktop entry, a plist key, `HKCU\Software\OpalaTex\MonitorsDataFolder`; a file written before that record belongs to the folder its `OPALATEX_HOME`, or its absence, resolves). Turning autostart off or on from another folder is refused with a diagnostic naming the owner — it once removed the service of the user's real data folder.
- **Two-level evaluation** (`spec.py`, `checks.py`, `engine.py`). Level 1 is deterministic: a check observes a value (an HTTP body or a `json_path`/`regex` extract, a command's exit code and output, a file's existence/mtime/hash), and the condition (`changed`, `equals`, `contains`, `regex`, `gt`/`ge`/`lt`/`le`, `delta`, `exit_code`, `exists`/`missing`, `always`) decides without a model. Level conditions fire on the rising edge, `delta` measures from the last firing (so each further move fires once), `changed` from the previous observation. Level 2 (`llm_check.py`) is an `llm` check: the project's worker model, read-only (`web_search` and a bounded page fetch), must answer a JSON verdict; anything else is a check error quoting it, never a guessed answer. It runs in a child process (`opalatex monitors llm-check`) because building an agent binds the project in module globals, and has a 5-minute minimum interval. Command checks obey the Docker sandbox exactly like `run_command` and never fall back to the host.
- **Lifecycle and loop breakers.** Past `expires_at` → `expired`; five consecutive check errors → `error` plus a critical notification; fired and not `repeat` → `completed`; `max_checks` used → `expired`. Status transitions (`completed`, `expired`) are recorded before the wake-up is queued, so the woken turn sees the monitor's post-firing status. A **task wait** (a monitor that wakes the chat it was registered from) also wakes that chat when it ends without firing, with the reason (`[Monitor event: exhausted|expired|error]`), so the orchestrator is never left waiting for a wake-up that will not come: it registers another monitor or says so in its reply. Any other monitor that ends gets a low-urgency notification instead; no chat is opened only to report an ending. **Repetition** (`repeat`) is the user's choice and is never overridden; unset, a task wait fires once and everything else (a notification, a new chat at every firing) keeps firing. It used to be forced to *once* for every monitor that wakes a chat, which made "a new chat every time" fire a single time. The type shown (*Task wait* / *Watch*) is derived the same way, from the action. **A continuing monitor is never re-registered by its wake-up** (`engine.wake_prompt`, `agent_tools._active_waking_monitor` / `_find_duplicate_active`): `wake_prompt` used to end unconditionally with "To keep waiting, register a new monitor with `register_monitor`", so an agent woken by a recurring monitor registered a copy on every cycle and even wrote that instruction into the copy's `resume_prompt`. `wake_prompt` now tells the agent when the monitor remains active and forbids calling `register_monitor` to renew it, offering `register_monitor` only when a task wait in the origin chat has ended; `register_monitor` itself refuses when the turn's waking monitor (`monitor_wake_id` / claimed wake request) is still `active`, or when an active monitor in the same project already matches `(name, check, condition, action.chat)`.
- **Which chat a wake-up runs in** (`action.chat`, `register_monitor(wake_in=...)`, the panel's *Wake a chat*): `origin` (the default) wakes the chat the monitor was registered from, whose conversation and saved orchestrator state carry the task on; `new` opens a chat in the monitor's project for every wake-up (`engine.create_wake_chat`, named `⏰ <monitor> · <time>`) and leaves the registering conversation untouched — for recurring work a watch triggers. The wake message then says it is a new chat and carries only the monitor's instructions, which must therefore be self-contained. The chat is created when the wake-up is queued (`wake_requests.new_chat` records it), so everything downstream (headless child, lease) handles a concrete chat id. **A new chat's wake-up never takes over the window**: the daemon always runs it in a child process, even when the window has the project open, because the window's server runs one turn at a time and a monitor working there would hold the chat the user is busy in (an earlier version switched the window to the new chat). The finished wake-up is an *event*: an OS notification ("New event from monitor …", the answer's start, a click opens the chat), an entry in the panel's *Recent events* with *Open chat*, and a count on the activity bar until the panel is opened (`wake_requests.result` / `seen`; `/api/monitors/events/seen`). *Clear* (`/api/monitors/events/clear`, `store.clear_events`) deletes the finished wake-ups only; a pending or running one is not an event yet, and the chats the events opened are kept. The window reloads its chat list when an event names a chat it does not know, reloads the history of the chat on screen when the event is in it, and refreshes the list before switching on *Open chat*. A wake-up naming no chat is refused when queued (`enqueue_wake`) and failed if one is found pending (`fail_unrunnable_wakes`): the window read an empty chat id as "the active chat" and ran it in whatever chat was open. A chat that cannot be created (the project is gone) is reported with an error event and a critical notification, never redirected; a deleted `origin` chat fails the wake-up the same way.
- **Registration fails fast** (`agent_tools.register`): the check runs once before anything is stored (except `timer` and `llm`), and an unreachable URL, an unmatched `json_path` or a non-numeric value for a numeric condition is returned as the diagnostic. The first observation becomes the baseline (`changed`, `delta`); a level condition that already holds is reported on the first scheduled check.
- **Orchestrator tools** (`agent_tools.build_monitor_tools`, added in `memgpt_runtime.build_chat_orchestrator`, guidance in `MONITORS_PROMPT_BLOCK`): `register_monitor` (flat arguments, not safe: `edit` asks, `plan` blocks — a monitor keeps reaching the network or running a command unattended), `list_monitors`, `pause_monitor`, `cancel_monitor` (safe). The orchestrator's working state is already saved per chat (§2.6), so a wake-up needs no snapshot of its own: the wake message is an ordinary turn that restores that state.
- **Who runs a wake-up.** The window polls `POST /api/monitors/poll` every 4 s (`hooks/useMonitorBridge.js`), which marks it alive for its open project. A wake-up of the *origin chat* of that project is claimed by the window when it is idle and run through `handleSendMessage` as a normal, streamed turn of its chat (off-screen chats keep their turn off-screen, as in §2.6), and counts as seen; a refused start returns it to the queue. Every other wake-up — any new chat's, and any project the window does not have open — is claimed by the daemon and run headless in a child, `opalatex monitors run-wake <id>` (`wake_runner.py`), through the same `agent_stdin.handle_run`, followed by a notification with the answer. **The headless turn runs on the `background` reply surface** (`agent_stdin.REPLY_SURFACE_BACKGROUND`, `prompt_surface.has_window`): the window tools (`open_file`, `set_layout`, `read_terminal`, …) and terminal-only commands are left out of its tools and prompt, and the desktop block ("the user is watching the same window") is replaced by one saying no window is open and the reply is read later in the desktop chat. It used to be offered tools that could only fail, and told the user was watching.
- **A monitor's turn asks nothing outside its chat** (`agent_stdin.unattended_turn`, `unattended_refusal`, `UnattendedTurnError`). Both runners mark the turn with `monitor_wake` (the window through `handleSendMessage({monitorWake})`, the headless child in its run data), and `handle_run` holds the mark for the turn. Every way a tool asks a person then refuses with a diagnostic the model receives, telling it to decide what it reasonably can and to put any question or approval it needs in its reply: tool permission in `edit` mode (the gate in `opalatex_tool`, at the point it would ask; "always allow" tools and safe listings still run), `ask_question`, `create_plan`, and `run_interactive_command` (which needs a person at a terminal in every mode). `request_user_input` raises for anything else, so no caller can wait on an answer that cannot come. The wake message says nobody is watching, and the registration guidance tells the orchestrator to settle details with the user before registering and to write a self-sufficient `resume_prompt`. **Superseded decision:** the first version stored such questions as pending inputs, notified the user and waited; in use, a vague `resume_prompt` produced six `ask_question` dialogs in a row outside the chat, so the channel was removed (the window's remote dialogs and badge, `opalatex monitors inputs|answer`, and the store's `pending_inputs`).
- **One turn per chat across processes** (`agent_stdin.handle_run` → `_take_turn_lease`): every orchestrator turn holds a lease on its (project, chat) in `monitors.db`; another live process's lease refuses the turn with a diagnostic, and a dead process's lease is taken over. The daemon does not dispatch a wake-up to a leased chat.
- **Notifications** (`notify.py`), delivered only by the daemon, which is the process that can receive the click. Linux: `org.freedesktop.Notifications.Notify` through `gdbus` with a *default* and an *Open chat* action, plus a long-lived `gdbus monitor` for `ActionInvoked` (`notify-send` without click where `gdbus` is absent; the snap stages `libglib2.0-bin`). Arguments are typed GVariant text (`uint32 0` for `replaces_id`, `@as []`, `<byte …>`, `int32 -1`) — a bare `-1` timeout is read by `gdbus` as an option, and under strict Snap confinement the `desktop` AppArmor plug denies `Introspect` on `/org/freedesktop/Notifications`, so `gdbus` parses an untyped `"0"` as `int32` instead of `uint32` and the server rejects `(sisssasa{sv}i)`. Windows: a WinRT toast from Windows PowerShell 5.1 under the `com.opalacoder.OpalaTex` AppUserModelID registered in `HKCU`, protocol-activated with `opalatex-monitor:open?project=…&chat=…`, a per-user scheme that runs `opalatex monitors open <uri>`; the XML travels base64-encoded. macOS: `osascript`, no click. A click queues an `open_requests` row; the window's poll consumes it, raises the window and switches project and chat, and with no window alive the app is started on that project.
- **The window's server never waits on the service manager** (`api.handle`, `agent_tools.register`, `pause_monitor`): `daemon_status`, `set_autostart`, `ensure_daemon_running` and the test notification run `systemctl`/`gdbus` and go through `asyncio.to_thread`; the panel asks for the status every 5 s, and each request used to run `systemctl` three times on the event loop. `SystemdUserAutostart.available` is probed once per 5 minutes.
- **Interfaces.** The window: the Monitors panel (activity bar, with the unseen-events count) shows *Recent events* and lists every project's monitors with type, trigger, last check/value/error and Pause/Resume, Test (a dry run that changes nothing), Cancel/Remove, per-monitor history, the daemon and login-service status, a test notification and a creation form. The terminal: `opalatex monitors list|info|add|pause|resume|cancel|delete|test|daemon|service|notify-test`, and `/monitors` in the REPL.

## 3. OpalaWebPage (Marketing Site)

`OpalaWebPage` (`www.opalacoder.com`) is now a static-content Express server: it serves the built React/Vite site and public assets (installer one-liners mirroring the README, the donation QR code, privacy policy). It holds no database, no user accounts, and makes no calls to any LLM provider. Its only integration with a third party is the PayPal donation link.

### 3.1 Donation Flow

1. The website and the desktop app's Settings > About expose the same official PayPal donation URL.
2. Both interfaces display the same QR Code asset supplied by the project owner.
3. PayPal processes the donation externally.
4. No account, key, or application state is created or changed by a donation — it is purely external to OpalaTex.

---

## 4. Current Product and Licensing Decisions

- OpalaTex is free and open-source under the MIT License.
- There is no trial, paid editor license, subscription, account, or local feature lock.
- AI features run through local Ollama models or through third-party providers (Gemini, OpenAI, Anthropic, etc.) configured with the user's own API key.
- The previously offered Opala Cloud AI proxy and paid token packages have been discontinued; OpalaTex has no paid application service.
- Donations fund open-source maintenance and are not purchases of any token, credit, or application feature.

## 5. Verification of Local Safety and Persistence

`tests/test_review_regressions.py`, `tests/test_diagnostics.py`, `tests/test_local_session.py`, and the dynamic-capability test in `tests/test_chat_turn_protocol.py` cover the plan, diagnostic, HTTP and sync contracts. `gui_src/src/utils/__tests__/fileSave.test.js` covers delayed/concurrent save responses. Run `.venv/bin/python gui_src/test/browser/local_session.py` from the repository root after building the frontend to check real Chromium startup, HttpOnly cookies, native assets and EventSource through both the packaged server and Vite; it uses a temporary application home and browser profile.

The POSIX terminal uses `selectors.DefaultSelector` instead of `select.select`, so a PTY with a descriptor above 1023 remains readable. The terminal regression suite exercises a real shell with an explicitly duplicated high descriptor.
